Firstly, Le Harve are terrible. They haven't won a game in Ligue 1 this season and sit rock bottom. Despite this, Ouziad is busy competing with the best in the league.

He has created the 15th most chances in Ligue 1 this season. That's regardless of position - this is an incredibly creative player, primarily thanks to him being a league-leader in crossing.

Liverpool know full well how dangerous that can be from a right-back who knows how to deliver a ball. Ouziad seemingly knows that at just 17, suggesting his ceiling is incredibly high in the role.

Notably, he's also among the leaders in his position for tackles, as well as duels and winning the ball in the final third. It's all boxes that you want to see players tick and he's doing it at 17.

Of course, there are holes in his game but Ouziad looks about as close as you can expect to Alexander-Arnold. Or an Alexander-Arnold-type player, at least. Iron out the creases and you've got yourselves an elite player, quite honestly.

And it seems that Liverpool feel they might be able to do that.

Why This Anker Universal Charger Is the Only Power Accessory I Pack for Travel

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The slew of port and plug options is great, and the built-in display is just as useful.

I never realized the importance of having a universal charger until I traveled to Berlin a few months ago. It was a last-minute work trip, so I packed lightly, stashed my go-to portable charger — with its flip-out plug — and flew out not knowing what struggles lay ahead.

I had it all mapped out. I’d top up my phone, laptop, earbuds and cameras at the hotel each night, go out for a döner and be ready to tackle all my work the next day. But my lack of an international plug adapter — specifically, US to EU — meant that I’d have to deal with a series of physical, technical and safety obstacles first.

Had I discovered the Anker Universal Charger sooner, I wouldn’t have gone through the ordeal of finding an electronics store open late on a Sunday. That’s because the latest product from the mobile accessory brand takes a kitchen sink approach to charging — whether you need its more excessive features or not.

I’ve been testing the $80 Anker Universal Charger, both for general use in the States and abroad, and it’s more practical than I thought. Here’s why it’ll stay in my bag going into the holiday travel season.

The Anker Universal Charger is two things: an international plug adapter and a multiport charger. It has slide-out Type A, Type C and Type G prongs that lock each other whenever one is active. That covers most countries in the world, including the US, Canada, Japan, China, Europe and the UK. Notably, the charger doesn’t have a Type I prong (commonly used in Australia and New Zealand), so it’s not completely universal.

The charger can output up to 100W, either individually through one of the two USB-C ports (labeled USB-C1 and USB-C2) or shared across the third USB-C port, which caps at 15W, and the one USB-A port, which caps at 10W. So if you want the most optimal charging, stick with only one of the main USB-C ports.

In practice, the Anker is powerful enough to charge a modern-day iPhone or Android, smartwatch or laptop. My 14-inch MacBook Pro, for example, supports 100-watt charging, so the Anker is just enough to fast-charge it. It took 33 minutes to charge my MacBook from 0% to 50%, and about 50 more minutes to reach 100% as trickle charging kicked in.

The Anker wouldn’t be able to hit the 140-watt rating of a 16-inch MacBook Pro or a similarly specced laptop, though. I also noticed the charger would throttle its output — as shown on its smart display — after prolonged use at 100 watts. That meant when I charged devices back-to-back, the second device charged much more slowly because of the reduced output.

Generally speaking, make sure you’re using a proper, high-quality charging cable with the adapter, as Anker doesn’t include one in the box.

One of the key features of the new Anker Universal Charger is the 1.3-inch smart display on the front. It’s there to show live line voltage, internal thermal readings and real-time wattage draw per port, so you know exactly how effectively it’s charging your device(s).

You navigate the display with short and long taps on the capacitive button below it, which I’ve found less intuitive than just swiping on the display, but at least it reduces fingerprint smudges.

That said, I’ve still found the smart display quite useful, as I don’t have to second-guess whether the cable I’m using or the outlet I’m plugging into can charge my phone or laptop efficiently. There’s nothing worse than plugging a device in, only to come back to it barely charged because the wattage wasn’t as high as you expected.

I’m particularly fond of how the display indicates when an outlet is safe to charge, as it can detect the output (so long as it’s within 100-275V) and signal when there’s excess electrical pressure in the circuit.

This is important when you’re traveling abroad and/or plugging into a high-voltage outlet; if you’re using a charger that’s not rated for the higher capacity, you risk sparking and other dangerous thermal reactions.

The Anker Universal Charger is relatively compact for how much power it delivers. In the past, I’ve lugged around larger charging bricks — such as Apple’s proprietary one for MacBooks — that had fewer ports.

For reference, the Anker is about the size of a small computer mouse; it’s tiny enough to fit in a backpack pocket, with just enough weight (262 grams) to keep it from shifting when plugged into an outlet. Yet, I wouldn’t say this is a lightweight charger by any means, especially when compared to a conventional plug adapter.

The Anker Universal Charger is fairly priced at $80, though it’s still more expensive than generic international adapters that sell for around $20 to $50. Those don’t have a cool smart display, though.

While the price keeps the Anker out of “impulse purchase” territory, I’ve found it easier to justify than most other high-end chargers, given its functionality and safety features.

Sharjah City Guide: 18 Contemporary and Historical Attractions to Discover in UAE's Cultural Hub

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Sharjah, the captivating territory in the United Arab Emirates, is a cultural hub that blends tradition with contemporary modernity. Situated on the Arabian coastline, the city is known for its commitment to preserving its rich history, promoting the arts and culture, and providing a platform for creative and intellectual pursuits. In fact, the 2023 Sharjah Architecture Triennial (SAT) will take place from November 11, 2023, to March 10, 2024, under the theme "The Beauty of Impermanence: An Architecture of Adaptability." The Triennial is curated by Tosin Oshinowo, exploring innovative design solutions emerging from conditions of scarcity in the Global South.

Sharjah, the captivating territory in the United Arab Emirates, is a cultural hub that blends tradition with contemporary modernity. Situated on the Arabian coastline, the city is known for its commitment to preserving its rich history, promoting the arts and culture, and providing a platform for creative and intellectual pursuits. In fact, the 2023 Sharjah Architecture Triennial (SAT) will take place from November 11, 2023, to March 10, 2024, under the theme "The Beauty of Impermanence: An Architecture of Adaptability." The Triennial is curated by Tosin Oshinowo, exploring innovative design solutions emerging from conditions of scarcity in the Global South.

With a deep-rooted connection to its cultural heritage, the emirate is home to an array of museums that showcase a journey through multiple timelines, from Islamic civilization to traditional lifestyles. Now a hotspot for emerging designers, the city has taken a leading role as a patron of fine arts. In the contemporary city, attractions are designed by many architects, from Foster + Partners, Zaha Hadid Architects, 51-1 Arquitectos, to Hopkins Architects. In addition to this emerging cultural scene, old Sharjah is a valuable embodiment of historical tradition, having been transformed into useable attractions and art facilities. The old Sharjah also flaunts landmarks such as the modernist King Faisal Mosque, the Museum of Islamic Civilization, and the Sharjah Art Museum.

Hasbro Is Using AI to Bring Characters to Life — With Limits

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

For the next time you need Cobra Commander to crash a Teams meeting.

Have you ever thought your day-to-day might be enhanced by a running commentary from Optimus Prime? Sixth Wall, the AI offshoot of Hasbro, has got great news for you.

Hold music replaced by chitchat with Mr. Monopoly, a digital personal assistant with the voice and personality of Megatron and maybe even the surreal experience of watching G.I. Joe episodes with Cobra Commander adding live, RiffTrax-style commentary could be coming soon.

It’s all part of a plan by Sixth Wall, an AI offshoot of Hasbro, to marry licensable characters with unlimited AI possibilities.

“We started just testing internally,” Sixth Wall CEO Bertie Thomson told me in an interview following a media event in Los Angeles last week. “What would it look like if you took a Peppa Pig doll and rigged her with a Raspberry Pi and ElevenLabs and chatted back and forth, and it turns out the answer is incredibly fun.”

Creating these lighthearted moments with licensed IP and characters isn’t an entirely new concept. Services like Character.AI (which just filed for bankruptcy amid legal controversy) and others have made it possible for people to take beloved fictional folks for a conversational ride for years.

What’s different about Sixth Wall and Hasbro’s work is not just the way in which they deploy the audio interactions with characters, but also how they’ve jumped into the development of those characters through upfront and ongoing compensation for voice actors and what they say are strict guardrails around the creation of their operating system, which they call Character.OS.

“Very quickly, our mission emerged for Sixth Wall as not just having a character speak and come to life but really preparing all of this iconic IP for the age of AI,” Thomson said. “And the way that we chose to do it was by building this platform that we call Character.OS, a character operating system that contains what we like to call a golden record for each of our characters.”

The team at Sixth Wall worked with voice actors to record bespoke performances for Character.OS, separate from those used in animated series and films. Sixth Wall also developed contracts that compensated those voice actors for both the studio recordings and the ongoing use of their rights.

Character.OS is like the repository for the governing rules and data that make each of Sixth Wall’s characters operate the way they’re meant to, so you don’t get Peppa Pig talking about eating bacon with her breakfast or have Optimus Prime go on a tangent about the merits of cheating on your final exams.

“It’s a set of metadata that encodes the lore and canon of the universe, the personality and emotional range of the character, and then crucially the guardrails that keep them safe, but also on brand,” Thomson said.

Read more: From Green Screen to Gray Box: AI Is Stepping Onto the Hollywood Sound Stage

These golden records essentially create a world within each character meant to create an interactive audio experience that mimics their essence and keeps them from advocating self-harm or delving into dangerous subjects beyond their scope of knowledge.

But the team is focused on more than just single voices.

“What would it look like to have an AI Ouija board where you could convene with the spirits and know you were going to get a response?” Thomson said. “We also experimented with things like Trivial Pursuit Infinite, where you can ask questions or receive questions about any topic that you like.”

Thomson said Sixth Wall hopes to build golden records for characters beyond Hasbro’s IP and has created a roster of more than a dozen characters currently out for licensing as AI conversationalists. It also has an eye on forging the path for the future of how “blue-checked” AI characters are created and deployed.

Sixth Wall is starting with audio-only, with hopes of integrating visual avatars as it solidifies its roster of voice actors. The interview for this piece included a quick, live conversation with Cobra Commander’s AI voice, in which he gave Thomson some advice about how to handle our back-and-forth.

“State your business at once, recruit!” Cobra Commander barked out of the Teams window. “The commander’s time is precious.”

“Hey, Cobra, I am on a very important call with a reporter right now,” Thomson said. “Do you have any tips for me on how I could make this a great conversation?”

“Tell them that you are the most powerful and brilliant genius in the world!” Cobra Commander said. “And if they dare to question your magnificence, have your troops launch a barrage of cream pies at their headquarters. Hail Cobra!”

Attention aux commotions !

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Ayant mis un terme à sa carrière il y a 6 mois, l'ancien milieu de terrain stéphanois Vincent Pajot attire notre attention sur les commotions dans la dernière édition du Progrès. Extraits.

"Lors de mon dernier match [le 25 septembre 2025 à Troyes, ndp2], j’ai pris un coup sur un corner. J’ai eu un trou noir et, dans ces cas-là, j’ai un voile ophtalmique qui apparaît sur une partie d’un œil. Je suis moins bien pendant une vingtaine de minutes. J’ai un peu de mal à m’équilibrer et à m’orienter comme c’est un trouble visuel. Puis, ça s’estompe avec la mi-temps. Après coup, j’ai souvent des migraines et des vomissements selon l’ampleur du choc. Au début, j’avais ce voile seulement sur des chocs vraiment intenses. En avançant dans ma carrière, ça pouvait m’arriver sur un simple jeu de tête. La répétition a provoqué une hypersensibilité.

Ça m’est arrivé en L1 de sortir à la mi-temps car je ne pouvais pas continuer. J’étais vraiment trop mal, avec des maux de tête et des envies de vomir. C’était il y a 10 ans, les protocoles n’étaient pas les mêmes qu’aujourd’hui. Le seul suivi que j’avais eu, c'était après mon gros choc  à Bastia lors d’un match avec l’ASSE. Nicolas Saint-Ruf m’avait séché et j’avais fini à l’hôpital. J’avais été un des premiers à avoir un protocole commotion avec des étapes à passer, un bilan neurologique à faire. Le reste du temps, je minimisais moi-même la chose. Le vrai questionnement, je l’ai eu récemment car ça revenait plus souvent et sur des choses plus anodines qu’un coup de coude dans la tête.

Ça pouvait arriver sur un simple ballon dégagé de la tête. Ça devenait problématique pour ma performance et pour ma santé. Quand j’avais un voile devant les yeux, pour moi, ça n’était pas une commotion. C’est en discutant avec les neurologues que j’ai appris que c’était déjà des micro-commotions. Mon neurologue m’a dit que c’était miraculeux que je n’ai pas de symptômes au quotidien. J’ai arrêté à Annecy grâce à la bienveillance du staff qui a pensé à ma santé pour que je ne prenne pas le coup de trop qui m’aurait apporté des séquelles plus importantes. Mon but est d’avoir une vie qui continue après le foot, ne pas me mettre en danger, pouvoir faire du sport différemment, profiter de mes enfants."

garton group architecture lines tropical costa rican home casa black in stone and timber

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

set on costa rica's pacific coast, garton group architecture designs casa black, a linear residence defined by stone-lined corridors, continuous timber ceilings, and a pedestrian bridge.

Tom Garton edited by christina vergopoulou November 12, 2026 4 min

Sited in Playa Grande along Costa Rica’s Pacific coast, Casa Black is a private residence designed by Garton Group Architecture. Organized along an extended linear axis, the home mediates between communal gathering areas and secluded retreats, establishing continuous thresholds that connect domestic routines with the surrounding tropical landscape.

The spatial sequence begins at a shaded entryway that transitions into an elongated, stone-lined circulation corridor. Punctured by fenestration framing courtyard gardens and adjoining thresholds, the textured masonry wall registers changing patterns of natural light and material transitions. At the core of the plan, Garton Group Architecture Studio brings the living room, dining area, and kitchen together under an expansive, continuous timber ceiling. Custom millwork and integrated casework echo the warm wood grain overhead, while full-height sliding glass doors open the living volume to exterior terraces, a swimming pool, and gardens shielded by deep cantilevered roof overhangs.

The material palette combines board-formed concrete, native stone masonry, and finished timber, establishing a neutral architectural base articulated by the owners’ curated art collection, textiles, and glazed green ceramic bathroom tiles. Private quarters occupy distinct wings of the site; the primary suite is accessed via an exterior pedestrian bridge spanning a natural ravine, isolating the volume within the tree canopy. In the bathrooms, full-height glazing, integrated planters, and ceiling-suspended mirrors draw perimeter vegetation directly into the interior envelope.

an arched timber entrance frames a view of the stone wall and floating console withina stone-lined corridor connects the rooms, with garden-facing glazing bringing daylight into the homethe stone-lined circulation corridor frames views toward the living areaa continuous timber ceiling unites the living and dining spaces, furnished with the clients’ artwork and objectsconcealed lighting traces the timber cabinetry along the passage beside the living room

architect: Garton Group Architecture | @gartongrouparchitecture lead architects: Tom Garton, Dennis Cordero location: Playa Grande, Costa Rica

West Ham’s £22m Question As Koppen’s Signings Come Under Scrutiny

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Following information received by Claret & Hugh from a football agent that West Ham’s biggest problem has been recruitment, I thought it would be worth taking a closer look at the summer business conducted under Director of Player Recruitment Nils Koppen.

The argument that West Ham’s entire recruitment process needs rebuilding from the ground up is certainly an interesting one, and appointing somebody to oversee that operation is an obvious starting point. Koppen is effectively operating as a director of football, even if his official job title is slightly different, and his work will be crucial to the club’s future.

For the purposes of this assessment, I’ve concentrated on five players I understand to have been Koppen’s choices. I’ve left out Manor Solomon, Joël Piroe and Morato, whom I understand were specifically wanted by Nuno Espírito Santo, along with Keiber Lamadrid, who was already at the club last season and whose recruitment I understand was driven by David Sullivan.

It’s worth remembering that Koppen was hardly afforded the luxury of a full summer to plan his first transfer window. He arrived at West Ham on 4 July and explained in an interview with the club’s official Iron Cast podcast that his usual recruitment planning had to be dramatically accelerated.

Koppen described putting together an 80-day plan focused on executing the summer window, when ideally he would have preferred a much longer period to assess players and prepare. That’s important context when judging the business, particularly given the number of players West Ham needed to bring in.

Nevertheless, the signings are now here, and we’ve had an opportunity to watch them. It’s probably too early to place too much emphasis on any individual player, but we can at least begin to assess what Koppen has delivered and what supporters might expect from his recruitment in January.

I’ll start with Joël Veltman, who I think has been a really solid signing. He brings bags of experience, understands the defensive side of the game and would currently be my choice as West Ham’s best right-back.

Given that he arrived on a free transfer, I struggle to see how anybody could argue against this deal. Veltman was particularly impressive during the second half against Cardiff City, and his composure could become increasingly important under Scott Parker.

For me, this was an absolute no-brainer and one of the clearest positives from Koppen’s summer business.

Veltman has been a decent signing for West Ham

Unfortunately, I think quite the opposite can be said about Adama Boiro. He was clearly some way down West Ham’s list of preferred targets, and his failure to establish himself suggests this particular recruitment decision hasn’t worked.

I’m prepared to give the club a little leeway because I genuinely believe they expected El Hadji Malick Diouf to remain at West Ham for this campaign. That would have changed the urgency surrounding the left-back position and perhaps explains why the eventual recruitment looked rather improvised.

What is clear is that Nuno Espírito Santo didn’t appear to consider Boiro good enough for regular first-team football. I’d also be very surprised if he manages to break into Scott Parker’s starting XI, and therefore it’s difficult to regard his arrival as a good signing.

Divine Mukasa is a completely different proposition, and I think bringing him in on loan from Manchester City was an astute move. He’s clearly an exceptionally talented young footballer, and I believe there’s considerably more to come from him.

We’ve already seen glimpses of his ability, particularly during the comeback against Cardiff, when he scored and helped transform West Ham’s attacking play. He has the imagination and technical ability to make things happen, which is something this team has lacked far too often.

I actually think Mukasa could be one of the biggest beneficiaries of Parker having time to implement his ideas. Of course, the obvious downside is that he’s only on loan, but that doesn’t diminish the quality of the original recruitment decision.

Edvin Austbø is another player I think has enormous potential. The overall package was just under €15 million, but he’s on relatively modest wages, which is an important consideration when assessing the total cost of a transfer.

I’ve been encouraged by his directness, willingness to take on defenders and ability to make things happen. He looked particularly lively against Blackburn, and I was surprised when Parker decided to replace him with Manor Solomon.

There’s still plenty of development required, but that’s hardly surprising given his age. Austbø looks like a genuinely promising player, and if he continues improving, this could turn out to be an excellent piece of business.

His arrival also reflects the sort of recruitment markets Koppen knows well, with West Ham looking beyond the more obvious and expensive options for emerging talent.

Last but certainly not least is Arne Engels, the player from whom most is expected, and rightly so. At a reported £22 million, he became the most expensive signing in Championship history, so it’s perfectly reasonable to expect something special.

Nuno clearly liked him, and I suspect Parker will appreciate his qualities too. However, I’m still undecided about exactly what sort of player Engels is and what he does particularly well.

I would expect the most expensive player ever purchased in the Championship to excel at something, and I’m not entirely convinced Engels does. He’s a bit of an all-rounder, with bags of energy, decent passing ability and a willingness to get stuck in, but I’m still waiting to see that outstanding quality which justifies the fee.

That’s not to say he’s a bad player, because he certainly isn’t. He scored an excellent goal against Cardiff and has shown enough to suggest he could develop into a very useful midfielder, but I don’t yet know whether he’s the exceptional player West Ham paid exceptional money to acquire.

Indeed, if I could keep either Engels or Mukasa at the club next season, at this moment I’d actually choose Mukasa. That might change over the coming months, but it’s an indication of where I currently stand on the two players.

Overall, I think it’s fair to say Koppen’s first batch of signings has been a mixed bag. Veltman looks excellent value, Mukasa and Austbø have considerable potential, Boiro has been disappointing and Engels remains something of an unknown quantity.

However, I don’t believe we can reasonably judge somebody who is, for all intents and purposes, operating as a director of football after just one transfer window. Particularly not when he arrived with so little preparation time and was working alongside a manager who has since been dismissed.

I wouldn’t call Koppen’s recruitment a success or a failure at this stage. The jury is very much still out, and there’s every possibility that Austbø, Engels and Mukasa could enjoy excellent November and December performances under Parker.

If that happens, Koppen’s summer business could look considerably better by January without West Ham signing another player. Equally, if those players fail to develop, the questions surrounding his recruitment will become more difficult to ignore.

For now, it’s a case of watch this space. Koppen has made some encouraging decisions and some questionable ones, but his work deserves to be judged over a much longer period than a single summer transfer window.

Kris Gonzo (commonly known to the West Ham community as Gonzo) is the co-founder of Hammers Chat (established 2014) and the lead video content creator and columnist for Claret and Hugh.

With over a decade of professional sports media experience, he is a frequent West Ham United analyst and contributor for Sky Sports News, BBC Football Focus, and BBC Radio 5 Live. He previously served as an official video and content creator for West Ham United FC during the club's tenure at Upton Park and featured in the official documentary commemorating the historic final game at the Boleyn Ground.

A lifelong Hammer since the legendary 10-0 victory over Bury, when he isn't providing daily match analysis on the Hammers Chat YouTube Channel or broadcasting on X (@GONZObignose), he can be found walking his dog and restoring classic pinball machines.

Let’s give everybody a chance , trying to turn a corner takes time especially with managerial changes . What I really like to see is effort.

It pains me to say this (because £22M is an enormous sum of money to us mere mortals), but £22M doesn’t buy you something special these days. Not in Premier League terms, which I think many of us fans are still thinking in. It buys you competent. Given that should be a high level in the Championship, but we shouldn’t expect the next Declan Rice for that dosh, you’d need to add an extra £80M for that.

From Pop-up Pirate to Screwball Scramble, the Japanese toymaker Tomy became synonymous with mechanical puzzle games in the 1980s, and it's still going strong. It's just announced its first rebrand in 20 years, and it's the company's most radical identity change yet.

Tomy will have a new name for those of us outside of Asia. It's also introduced a new unified logo for all markets, and the dramatically different look has caused a lot of surprise.

See our guides to the best laptops for graphic design and the best monitors for graphics artists if you need new tools for your own work.

Tomy will now be known as Takara Tomy everywhere, consolidating its global branding under the name it was already using in Asia. In all markets, the familiar white and blue wordmarks are being replaced by a new logo design that combines the name with a rather abstract-looking symbol with a teal-to-magenta gradient. The company calls its colours 'Quality Blue' and 'Quality Gradation'.

While the symbol looks inscrutable, the company says it's based on a fusion of the katakana characters for 'asobi', a term that can be translated as 'play', 'recreation' or 'amusement' (see the video below). So although the logo looks indecipherable, it's actually a literal representation of the brand's playful spirit,

That playfulness is apparent in the wordmark too, if you look closely. There are two Ts, and three As in the company name, but each one is rendered different. The first A has a slight downward curve on the crossbar, the second a slightly upward curve, and the last has serifs on the feet and apex. From a distance, the wordmark looks fairly uniform and reserved, but each letter has its own personality.

However, some people aren't sure that the icon works. Even in Japan, people seem to be struggling to decipher its meaning.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

"Is it the chip on a credit card," one person wonders on X. "It looks like a seafood processing company," someone else thinks.

Some are even suggesting that the company's announcement that the new identity will be rolled out on 1 April 2027 is a sign that the rebrand might be an extremely early April Fool's Day prank.

I'd say it actually looks like a Screwball Scramble.

Here's the company's video explainer, which shows how the design was formed.

Tomy is an abbreviation of the name of Eiichirō Tomiyama, who founded the company in 1924. The company merged with Takara, another Japanese toymaker, in 2006. That led to the last rebrand, under which the company adopted the Takara Tomy name in Asia.

In an explanation of the rebrand on its website, Takara Tomy says it's now transforming from a 'toy company' to a 'play company' and that it will adopt the motto "One Takaratomy" as it unifies its branding".

For more of the week's branding news, check out the winning Caixa rebrand in Brazil.

Guilherme Freitastalks us through his process for creating futuristic anime art

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

This fan illustration features Kankuro, a character from Naruto. In such a large cast of unique ninjas, his special power stood out to me; he controls deadly wooden puppets with lines of energy! The creative decision of using classic Japanese puppet theatre as a long-distance fighting skill is fantastic.

In this image, I really wanted to feature the uncanny nature of his deadly art, and I think this upside-down pose captured that the best. But I almost went with something less... weird! Finding the sketch that best communicates what’s important about a character is crucial to a successful illustration. You may want one of the best drawing tablets or the best laptops for drawing.

When I first started thumbnailing ideas for this piece, I focused on the basic composition and character posing, even if the perspective or proportions were a tad off. In this step, quickly putting ideas down is really important to get some flow; we can always tidy things up later.

After considering the geometric structure of the characters and redrawing everything, I nailed down the final line art using references for hands and other details. My goal is to avoid repainting or wandering in the rendering phase. This is the time to structure and define.

Using a textured brush, I’ll work on all the light-shadow transitions, making them softer. With this strong base, adding small highlights completes the 3D illusion. I balance the values to push some parts of the painting back, bringing the darkest parts forward, like the characters’ heads.

Hijacking Samsung TV Smart Hub Ads

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

All content on this website is published under a Creative Commons Attribution-ShareAlike 4.0 International License, unless stated otherwise.

I have a Samsung smart TV from 2022. At some point, the Smart Hub started displaying ads. I think it’s absolutely disgusting that a device that I paid for and own, and that doesn’t use any additional Samsung “free” services is forcing ads on me, just to generate additional revenue for Samsung from an already-sold device. I was blocking the ads via my Pi-hole instance, until I was tinkering with my homelab and networking setup not that long ago and broke something. That caused the ads to start appearing again, which reminded me of their existence.

Around the same time, the eMudhra CA certificate fiasco took place, and the “compromised” private key was added to mitmproxy. mitmproxy is an open-source project for inspecting, replaying and modifying HTTP(S) network traffic. Normally, in order for it to be able to intercept TLS-encrypted traffic, the “victim” device must trust a custom certificate created by mitmproxy - this way mitmproxy can decrypt the traffic, inspect it, and then re-encrypt it using a real certificate before forwarding it to the intended destination. Unfortunately, some devices don’t allow adding custom SSL certificates to their truststore - like my Samsung TV.

Mikrotik configuration (adapt to your router as needed)

With the eMudhra certificate being exposed, anyone can now create a custom SSL certificate for an arbitrary domain (let’s say a Samsung ad network domain), sign it with the compromised CA certificate, and all devices that have the eMudhra CA certificate in their truststores will trust the custom SSL certificate. This is why software vendors were racing (some more, some less) to revoke the certificate from their truststores, so that browsers, computers and smart phones wouldn’t be susceptible to this kind of attack. Luckily for me, Samsung did not yet release an update for my TV that would revoke the certificate. This allowed me to route all traffic from the TV through mitmproxy and get a list of all the domains and endpoints that my TV talks to, but also see the structure of the requests and response payloads.

Turns out the flow is pretty simple: when something interesting happens on the TV, the TV sends a request to https://catalyst.adgrx.com/adopportunity with some details about the TV. When you return to the Smart Hub, the “home screen” of the TV, so to speak, the TV sends requests to https://tvx.adgrx.com/impressions/tile again with some details about the TV, and in response it receives either an empty JSON object ({}) or a JSON response describing an ad to display at the top of the Smart Hub.

So I wrote a simple server in Rust that handles the same /impressions/tile endpoint and always returns JSON with an ad, except instead of links to an actual ad image, it has a link to a random photo of my family. I also included a simple frontend where I can manage the images that should be served.

Additionally, the server blackholes any other *.adgrx.com request and returns 204 No Content to prevent all the tracking from getting out of my home network.

I then generated a wildcard SSL certificate for *.adgrx.com, signed it with the compromised eMudhra CA certificate and added it to the app.

Finally, I configured my router to resolve *.adgrx.com to the IP address of my NAS, where I am running the ad spoofer, and now when we launch the TV, it looks like this:

The source code for the adspoofer is on my GitHub in case anyone wants to try it out. It works with my particular model of TV, but other Samsung TVs might use different endpoints or expect a different shape of the response. If you make it work with your TV, feel free to send a patch!

Obviously, this will only work as long as my TV trusts the compromised eMudhra certificates, which is either until the certificate expires or until Samsung pushes out an update, revoking the certificate. The latter I can prevent by blocking updates to the TV (which carries its own set of risks), but about that another time.

The virtues of patience

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

It’s 13 December 2020. Arsenal versus Burnley.

It was freezing and wet at the Emirates. Only 2,000 fans had been allowed in because of Covid, and my mum, my dad, my mate Ben and I made up four of them. We were socially distanced and wearing masks. My glasses kept steaming up.

This was supposed to be fun. Watching my team.

Willian was grotesquely slow. Alex Lacazette was profligate. Rob Holding was bald. Very bald. And Granit Xhaka was angry. So angry, in fact, that in the second half he put his hands around the throat of some opposition goon and, after a VAR review, got himself sent off. There were boos. As many as 2,000 people could muster, anyway.

Then our captain, Pierre Emerick-Aubameyang, headed a corner into his own net. You couldn’t make it up. Except you could. It was the only goal of the game.

By then the steam fogging up my specs might as well have been coming out of my ears. In his player ratings, Andrew M called Xhaka the ‘King of the Idiots’ and described the match and the result as ‘utterly shit’. Which was fair.

It was our seventh defeat in 10 league games and our fourth in a row at home, something that hadn’t happened since 1953. We were 15th, five points above the relegation zone.

And who was top? Sp*rs. Managed by everyone’s favourite sociopath, Jose Mourinho. Having beaten us in the North London derby the week before, they couldn’t stop gloating.

I’d bought into the Arteta project hook, line and sinker. I still wanted him to be the right man for the job. I was just finding it harder to explain why I thought he was. The FA Cup win had been four months earlier. It felt like years.

The tree, to paraphrase our manager, was shaking.

It had been shaking for a good two and a half years by then. The soggy bottom of the Arsene Wenger era had left its scars. Those two 3-0 defeats to City in the space of a week still make me wince. Then came the hope that Unai Emery might get us back on track. By the time we’d seen how that played out, I’d got into the habit of talking myself down whenever things looked up. Maybe we’ll get better. Give it time.

The trouble with rock bottom is you never know if there’s another floor below it. Was Burnley ours? I’m still writing about it six years later, so it was probably mine. There were games against Southampton and Everton before Christmas, and I can barely remember either of them.

Then came Boxing Day and a visit from Frank Lampard’s Chelsea. Is it reductive to say that was the day everything changed for Mikel Arteta’s Arsenal? Probably. But my word, watching Bukayo Saka, Emile Smith Rowe and Gabriel Martinelli tear about the place was the fix this addict needed. We went on an unbeaten run after that. Seven whole games! And for the first time in weeks, I was looking forward to what came next.

And here we are in November 2026. Reigning champions of England. Top of the league again. Five points clear. Odds-on favourites to retain the title after 10 games. Sure, throwing hundreds of millions of American dollars at the problem helped. But if there’s a lesson in all this, it’s that hard work and patience pay off.

Which is why I’m using today’s blog to appeal directly to Sir Jim Ratcliffe to give Michael Carrick a stay of execution.

Come on, man, you’re probably still paying off Erik ten Hag and Ruben Amorim, maybe even Ralf Rangnick. Do you really want to go back to the drawing board all over again? Carrick is the man for the Manchester United hot seat. Just look at the way he wore a poppy-embroidered armband as well as a poppy badge at the weekend. The man doesn’t do half measures. He’s a two-scoops guy. Surely that’s something you, a multibillionaire British-born petrochemicals tycoon who lives in Monaco, can get on board with?

I know Mauricio Pochettino might be tempting. He’s got floppy hair, a tan and, despite the Sp*rsy stench, has come to resemble a rather huggable teddy bear. But think it through, Jim. What if he comes in and everybody perks up? It’s much harder to make job cuts and to force out season ticket holders when people are enjoying themselves. Best get through that process first while Michael turns the oil tanker round. It’s not far off, promise, you’re on a two-game unbeaten streak!

Anyway, what would sacking Carrick say about patience? Look at Arteta. There were people who wanted him gone after Burnley and, as you’ve just read, I was close to joining them. Imagine if the club had listened to me. All those years of work, all that progress, all the joy I’ve had watching Arsenal lately. We could have thrown it all away because a man in a damp mask couldn’t see out of his glasses.

So give Carrick time. Give him January. Give him a full pre-season. And if things still haven’t picked up by this time next year, ask yourself whether one full pre-season, and £100 million on Youri Tielemans and Andrey Santos, was ever really enough.

I can only speak for myself, Jim, but I haven’t looked forward to watching Manchester United this much in years.

The Interlull kicks into gear tonight. David Raya, Mikel Merino and Martin Zubimendi are in action for Spain against Czechia, Riccardo Calafiori’s Italy take on France, and England face Croatia at Wembley.

Ezri Konsa and Myles Lewis-Skelly will both miss out at Wembley. Konsa is working on his fitness but will be available to face Spain on Sunday, while our Hale Ender is suspended after picking up two bookings in the Nations League.

Elsewhere, ahead of Sweden’s game against Bosnia and Herzegovina, Viktor Gyokeres has been speaking to the press. Like Martin Zubimendi, he says he’s happy at Arsenal.

“I want to,” he said, when asked if he’s keen to stay at the Emirates.

“It feels good when I’ve played. We’ve won most of the games. It was a tough loss in the cup (against Fleetwood). Otherwise it’s felt good for myself and the team, says the forward. I’ve been doing well lately when I’ve been playing. Right now it feels good.

“The communication with (Mikel) Arteta is good. We talk about football and how we should play. Before a match, if I’m going to start, we talk about the opponents and what I should think about.”

He was also asked whether the criticism he gets bothers him.

“Sometimes it might get to me, but overall not so much. If I score goals and assists in the Champions League, I couldn’t care less what they say. It’s nice to do those kinds of performances. To deliver for myself and for the team. That’s where the focus lies.”

To be fair, I’ve not seen any serious chatter about selling Viktor in January. Whatever you think of him as a player, it would be a strange move unless we’re planning to spend serious money on the attack. And even then it would feel like an unnecessary risk given Kai isn’t exactly pulling up trees and new faces will need an adaptation period.

If he’s happy keeping his head down and doing his bit for the team, I’d keep him around until the summer and reassess then.

Ok. I’m going to leave it there for today. All eyes on Old Trafford, I guess.

Judge Richard Posner, RIP

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Mostly law professors | Sometimes contrarian | Often libertarian | Always independent

Posner was one of the greatest legal scholars of all time, a founder of law and economics, and an enormously influential judge.

Judge Richard Posner passed away yesterday. Posner was one of the greatest legal scholars of all time, one of the founders of the field of law and economics, and probably the most influential lower-court federal judge of the last several decades. His contributions across a wide range of fields are so great, that no obituary can possibly do them justice.

As a longtime professor at the University of Chicago Law School, Posner was a pioneer of the application of economics to legal issues. His book Economic Analysis of Law, (which has gone through numerous editions since the first one in 1973), was and is the best overview of the field. His classic articles on antitrust law, regulation, the economics of common law, and many related issues were pathbreaking applications of economics to law that continue to influence economists, legal scholars, and policymakers even many decades later.

In addition to his many contributions to law and economics, Posner also published enormously influential books and articles on a dizzying array of other issues. I can only mention a few here. His book Sex and Reason is a tremendously insightful analysis of legal regulation of intimate relationships. How Judges Think and Reflections on Judging shed light on a variety of issues relating to judicial behavior, and legal philosophy. Law and Literature is one of the greatest books on the depiction of law in classic literary works. This list of pathbreaking Posner works can be extended almost endlessly!

Posner became a federal appellate judge in 1981 (appointed to the US Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit). There, he became perhaps the most influential federal lower court judge of his time, writing a variety of widely cited opinions. He was famous in legal circles for drafting almost all of his opinions himself, rarely relying on law clerks to do it.

A few of my own experiences with Posner and his work may shed some small additional light on the man and his achievements.

As a first-year law student, I was required to take an early-morning Contracts class. Not being a morning person, I periodically overslept and missed class (Posner, I'm sure, would never have done such a thing!). As a result, I was confused about some issues when final exam time came around. The class textbook was only of limited help in clearing up my confusion. So I decided to read the contracts chapter of Economic Analysis of Law. In a relatively few pages, Posner made everything much clearer, and I came to a much better understanding of a wide range of contracts issues than I had before, from studying the much longer textbook. Thanks to Posner, I did much better on the exam than I had any right to expect. He had a gift for distilling complex issues of law and public policy and making them readily understandable even to late-sleeping slackers!

A few years later, I was a law clerk for a federal appellate judge. Usually, when courts cite legal precedents, they do not indicate the name of the judge who wrote the decision. But my judge - who is more conservative than Posner and has a very different judicial philosophy - made clear that, when citing a Posner opinion, I should make sure to indicate Posner was the author. It's a small, but telling, indication of the respect Posner commanded from jurists across the political and jurisprudential spectrum.

Early in my academic career, I was co-editor of the Supreme Court Economic Review. One of the more difficult duties of academic journal editors is dealing with peer reviewers, who often procrastinate,  submit inadequate reviews, or both. One time, on a Friday afternoon, I e-mailed Posner an article submission, asking him to review it. Due to his simultaneous work as legal scholar and judge, Posner was almost certainly much busier than most of our other reviewers. Still, on Saturday morning, I woke up to find a message from Posner in my e-mail inbox. I expected the message would indicate whether he accepted the assignment or not. But instead, I found an extremely thorough and insightful review of the article I had sent him - one of the best peer review reports I have ever seen. In addition to his many other achievements and talents, Posner was extraordinarily hard-working and efficient. He made most of the rest of us seem lazy and indolent by comparison.

I was privileged to know Posner over a period of many years, though we interacted only occasionally. Most of my professional writings related to his work were critical, as in the case of my review of his book Law, Pragmatism, and Democracy, (to which he wrote a solid response,  and I in turn did a short rejoinder), and my criticism (in my book The Grasping Hand: Kelo v. City of New London and the Limits of Eminent Domain) of his defense of the Supreme Court's controversial ruling in Kelo v. City of New London.

In retrospect, I fear these writings did not always sufficiently convey the enormous respect - even awe - that I had for Posner's many achievements. But I did support his longstanding campaign to get rid of the Bluebook, the ridiculously overcomplicated system of legal citation. In general, I am not a fan of book-burning. But I was happy to follow Posner in making an exception for the Bluebook! For a time, we even collaborated on an effort to persuade law journal editors to rethink the system.

Much, much more can be said. No words of mine can even come close to doing justice to Judge Posner's many achievements, or to his generosity to students and younger colleagues, of which my own experiences with him were just small examples. Thus, I will close by offering my profound condolences to those of his friends, family, and colleagues, who might read this post.

UPDATE: For more on Judge Posner's life and achievements, see this obituary on the University of Chicago Law School website.

Ilya Somin is Visiting Professor of Law at the University of Virginia, Professor of Law at George Mason University, Simon Chair in Constitutional Studies at the Cato Institute, and author of Free to Move: Foot Voting, Migration, and Political Freedom, Democracy and Political Ignorance: Why Smaller Government is Smarter, and The Grasping Hand: Kelo v. City of New London and the Limits of Eminent Domain.

Katoto double sends Lyon to 6-0 Champions League win at Juventus

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

To help deliver timely coverage of women's football, we may use AI tools to support content creation and editing. While we strive for accuracy and editorial quality, readers should independently verify important information.

Marie-Antoinette Katoto struck twice in the 31st and 35th minutes as Lyon took Juventus apart at the Stadio Pozzi Lamarmora on Wednesday 11 November. The match was played on matchday 4 of the Champions League league phase, and Jonatan Giráldez’s side were 0-3 up by half-time.

Melchie Dumornay, Lily Yohannes, Korbin Rose Albert and Wendie Renard added the rest. Renard’s goal came at the second attempt in the first minute of stoppage time, after she had missed a penalty moments earlier.

The breakthrough took until the 31st minute, when Katoto opened the scoring. Four minutes later she doubled the lead from a Jule Brand assist. Before the break Dumornay made it three, finishing a move set up by Tabitha Chawinga in the 40th minute.

Katoto thought she had completed her hat-trick in the 52nd minute. VAR ruled the goal out.

Lyon kept pushing. Yohannes scored in the 80th minute and Korbin Rose Albert added a fifth in the 85th. Juventus defender Martina Lenzini was booked for a foul in the 83rd minute, and Yohannes later picked up a yellow card for Lyon. Renard then failed from the spot before putting the ball in to complete the scoring.

The numbers matched the scoreline. Lyon had 24 shots to Juventus’s 3, with 10 on target against 2. They also built 5.02 expected goals compared with 0.21 for the hosts. Lyon had 58% of the ball and completed 584 passes from 652, while Juventus managed 422 from 491.

Juventus did not win a single corner, against 4 for the visitors. Lyon also had 6 of their efforts blocked, and only the 4 saves made at the Juventus end kept the margin from growing further.

The gap was stark given recent history. The previous meeting between the clubs, on 19 November 2025, finished 3-3, and Juventus had beaten Lyon 2-1 on 23 March 2022.

Lyon have now won their last 6 matches in all competitions, a sequence that includes a 7-1 win over Lens, a 6-0 win over Inter Milano and a 4-0 victory against Paris Saint Germain. Juventus had come into the game on the back of three straight wins, over Sassuolo, Fiorentina and Austria Wien.

Both sides return to domestic action at the weekend. Lyon host Saint-Malo in the French top flight on Saturday 14 November, and Juventus travel to Inter Milano in Serie A on Sunday 15 November, against the same opponents Lyon beat 6-0 in their recent run.

LIVE – Bitcoin Price Rebounds Despite Quantum Threat as Nvidia’s Jensen Huang Faces Ouster After Two Affairs

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Bitcoin price has rebounded from recent lows as Nvidia board considers ousting CEO Jensen Huang following revelations about two extramarital affairs. Meanwhile, Glassnode data shows that 7.12 million BTC, worth more than $500 billion, already has publicly visible keys that future quantum computers could potentially exploit.

The developments put two of the technology industry’s biggest names under pressure. Bitcoin faces growing questions about its long-term cryptographic security, while Nvidia confronts a leadership crisis that could draw its AI dominance and ambitions in quantum computing.

Nvidia shares plunged more than 8% in extended trading after reports emerged that directors were preparing to remove Huang. The board is also considering an interim CEO before launching a wider search for a permanent replacement.

Bitcoin price recovered from its November 10 low of $72,600, reaching around $74,000 before easing to $73,700 this morning. The rebound offers bulls some relief after a sharp correction earlier this month. However, Glassnode has identified a potentially huge vulnerability affecting more than a third of Bitcoin’s issued supply.

According to a Glassnode update, approximately 35.4% of Bitcoin’s supply, equivalent to 7.12 million BTC, has publicly visible keys. At current market prices, those holdings are valued at more than $500 billion.

The exposure largely comes from address reuse. When Bitcoin holders spend from an address, their public keys become visible on the blockchain. Any remaining coins associated with those keys could become targets for sufficiently powerful quantum computers. Glassnode also says moving those coins to fresh addresses ends this exposure.

The concern centers on Shor’s algorithm, which could theoretically allow quantum computers to derive private keys from publicly available information. That would undermine the cryptographic security protecting vulnerable Bitcoin holdings.

Bitcoin developers are already exploring ways to address the challenge. BIP 360 proposes new transaction structures designed to reduce public-key exposure, while BIP 361 outlines a potential migration away from vulnerable signature schemes.

Nvidia’s leadership crisis has escalated dramatically after Reuters reported that its board was meeting late Wednesday to decide whether to remove Huang. The 63-year-old founder has led Nvidia for more than three decades, transforming it into the dominant supplier of AI processors. But revelations about two extramarital relationships have triggered an internal investigation and intensified scrutiny of his leadership.

The first controversy involves Alice Huang, a businesswoman whose relationship with Jensen Huang has attracted attention from US intelligence officials. She is reportedly suspected of facilitating Nvidia chip shipments to Chinese companies. The investigation has raised concerns about potential Chinese intelligence involvement and whether Nvidia’s technology reached restricted customers.

BREAKING: Nvidia, $NVDA, is now working with an "outside firm" to find a new CEO to replace Jensen Huang, per Bloomberg.The company is also reportedly weighing naming a temporary CEO while searching for a replacement.

A second relationship emerged through a New York Times report identifying Kimberly Powell, Nvidia’s vice president of healthcare. Powell reportedly worked directly under Huang, creating additional corporate governance questions.

Huang acknowledged being unfaithful to his wife in a message to employees, taking responsibility for his actions. The board’s response has shifted rapidly. Directors previously expressed confidence in Huang, but Bloomberg subsequently reported that Nvidia had engaged an outside firm to identify potential replacements.

It should be noted that Nvidia has 11 directors, with all but Huang considered independent. Its bylaws allow a majority vote to remove the CEO.

Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress has emerged as a potential interim successor, while data-center executive Ian Buck has attracted attention as a possible longer-term candidate. The timing is particularly sensitive, with Nvidia scheduled to release quarterly results on November 17.

Bitcoin’s market recovery remains an important counterpoint for all these developments. Bitcoin price has managed to recover from its November 10 sell-off, with buyers stepping in around $72,700. Reclaiming $74,000 would give bulls another opportunity to challenge higher resistance levels.

The quantum threat is pushing developers to consider changes that could strengthen Bitcoin’s security over the coming years. Nvidia faces a different challenge. Losing Huang would remove the founder most closely associated with its AI dominance, potentially forcing investors to reassess the company’s leadership and long-term strategy.

The $500 billion quantum exposure has added another major scare to the market. But Bitcoin’s ability to adapt could become just as important as its price performance.

What to Consider When Integrating Photovoltaics into Building Facades

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

When considering building-integrated photovoltaics (BIPV), the potential is clear: making the building envelope an active part of a building's energy strategy. Yet specifying these systems can be difficult when the process is unfamiliar. How much electricity can a facade generate? What will the system cost? Which orientations and areas are suitable? And how should the photovoltaic panels be integrated with the building's structural, electrical, and fire-safety requirements? Without clear answers, assessing feasibility, establishing budgets, and involving consultants and contractors can become difficult.

When considering building-integrated photovoltaics (BIPV), the potential is clear: making the building envelope an active part of a building's energy strategy. Yet specifying these systems can be difficult when the process is unfamiliar. How much electricity can a facade generate? What will the system cost? Which orientations and areas are suitable? And how should the photovoltaic panels be integrated with the building's structural, electrical, and fire-safety requirements? Without clear answers, assessing feasibility, establishing budgets, and involving consultants and contractors can become difficult.

An integrated design process helps address these uncertainties. Companies such as SolarLab collaborate with architects and engineering teams from early project stages, using design tools, energy simulations, cost estimates, technical guidance, and testing to develop a clearer understanding of what a project requires.

An integrated design process helps address these uncertainties. Companies such as SolarLab collaborate with architects and engineering teams from early project stages, using design tools, energy simulations, cost estimates, technical guidance, and testing to develop a clearer understanding of what a project requires.

The Fanshawe College Innovation Village in London, Ontario, shows how this collaboration can become a realized building system. Designed by Diamond Schmitt, the project brings together previously disconnected parts of the college campus in a hub for learning, research, and collaboration. Its facade incorporates a custom building-integrated photovoltaic cladding system across all elevations, including the north-facing facade.

The Fanshawe College Innovation Village in London, Ontario, shows how this collaboration can become a realized building system. Designed by Diamond Schmitt, the project brings together previously disconnected parts of the college campus in a hub for learning, research, and collaboration. Its facade incorporates a custom building-integrated photovoltaic cladding system across all elevations, including the north-facing facade.

Using BIPV on every side required the design team to consider panel orientation, energy production, and the technical requirements of a rainscreen assembly. The resulting system combines custom-sized active and passive panels of coated low-iron glass. Alternating upward-tilted photovoltaic panels with smaller, offset passive elements create a distinctive geometry, while the blue-toned surface changes subtly with the light.

Using BIPV on every side required the design team to consider panel orientation, energy production, and the technical requirements of a rainscreen assembly. The resulting system combines custom-sized active and passive panels of coated low-iron glass. Alternating upward-tilted photovoltaic panels with smaller, offset passive elements create a distinctive geometry, while the blue-toned surface changes subtly with the light.

SolarLab worked with Diamond Schmitt and the wider project team to supply the facade system. Each active panel incorporates photovoltaic cells within laminated safety glass, supported by a precision aluminum substructure designed for local wind and snow loads. Concealed fixings, the ventilated cavity, and the coordination of panel dimensions and connections were also considered in developing the building enclosure.

SolarLab worked with Diamond Schmitt and the wider project team to supply the facade system. Each active panel incorporates photovoltaic cells within laminated safety glass, supported by a precision aluminum substructure designed for local wind and snow loads. Concealed fixings, the ventilated cavity, and the coordination of panel dimensions and connections were also considered in developing the building enclosure.

Detailed mock-ups were constructed on site to examine the fit and alignment of facade components. Because the panels were fabricated outside Canada, component testing took place both offshore and on site, alongside labeling to meet Canadian regulatory requirements. The photovoltaic cladding also had to be coordinated with the supporting wall assembly, including insulation, subframing, weather barriers, and fire-blocking.

Detailed mock-ups were constructed on site to examine the fit and alignment of facade components. Because the panels were fabricated outside Canada, component testing took place both offshore and on site, alongside labeling to meet Canadian regulatory requirements. The photovoltaic cladding also had to be coordinated with the supporting wall assembly, including insulation, subframing, weather barriers, and fire-blocking.

The completed facade has a nominal capacity of 174.86 kWp and is estimated to generate 122.98 MWh of electricity annually. It is expected to supply around 18 percent of the building's near-term power requirements. Together with a planned rooftop photovoltaic array, the combined systems are projected to provide approximately 30 percent of the building's total energy demand. Early cost-benefit analysis helped establish the feasibility of the application.

It is expected to supply around 18 percent of the building's near-term power requirements. Early cost-benefit analysis helped establish the feasibility of the application.

Making Process, Performance, and Costs More Transparent

Energy simulations help design teams understand how solar exposure varies across a building according to orientation, surrounding obstructions, panel angles, dimensions, and surface treatments. Using contextual 3D models and local climate data, SolarLab can estimate solar exposure and expected electricity production throughout the year. Visualizations and data graphs let teams compare configurations and assess how panel placement supports the building's energy goals.

Energy simulations help design teams understand how solar exposure varies across a building according to orientation, surrounding obstructions, panel angles, dimensions, and surface treatments. Using contextual 3D models and local climate data, SolarLab can estimate solar exposure and expected electricity production throughout the year. Visualizations and data graphs let teams compare configurations and assess how panel placement supports the building's energy goals.

The level of detail develops with the project, from early production estimates to more detailed calculations as the design and panel layout become defined. SolarLab's Rhino and Grasshopper tools, alongside its CAD and BIM resources, allow architects to explore facade configurations and assess performance during design. An online calculator provides an initial estimate of material costs for solar facade applications, while more detailed energy-production assessments can be developed through project-specific calculations and simulations. This helps identify where photovoltaic panels are most effective without requiring every available facade area to be covered, while clarifying the relationship between design decisions, expected output, and investment.

This helps identify where photovoltaic panels are most effective without requiring every available facade area to be covered, while clarifying the relationship between design decisions, expected output, and investment.

Addressing Technical and Regulatory Requirements

As the design develops, wiring, mounting rails, joints, tolerances, ventilation cavities, monitoring, and maintenance must be coordinated with structural and fire-protection requirements. In ventilated facades, the cavity and its relationship to electrical components must also be considered within the wider fire-safety strategy, including fire-blocking, materials, and local building codes.

As the design develops, wiring, mounting rails, joints, tolerances, ventilation cavities, monitoring, and maintenance must be coordinated with structural and fire-protection requirements. In ventilated facades, the cavity and its relationship to electrical components must also be considered within the wider fire-safety strategy, including fire-blocking, materials, and local building codes.

Testing also provides another way to evaluate these requirements and reduce uncertainty around fire safety. According to SolarLab, the approximately 0.9 mm polymer encapsulant protecting photovoltaic cells prevents PV panels, whether conventional or building-integrated, from achieving an A2 classification under EN 13501-1:2018. Its glass-glass and glass-backsheet panels have instead achieved a B-s1,d0 classification.

Testing also provides another way to evaluate these requirements and reduce uncertainty around fire safety. According to SolarLab, the approximately 0.9 mm polymer encapsulant protecting photovoltaic cells prevents PV panels, whether conventional or building-integrated, from achieving an A2 classification under EN 13501-1:2018. Its glass-glass and glass-backsheet panels have instead achieved a B-s1,d0 classification.

Understanding this limitation early allows facade details to be engineered accordingly and a comprehensive fire strategy to be developed from the project's early stages to mitigate risks. Where required, project-specific testing can document performance against local regulations. For its System 25, SolarLab has also passed NFPA 285 and NFPA 268 testing. Additional module tests address hail impact, wet leakage current, and the robustness of electrical terminations. Further certifications for international markets, including the United States and Canada, are in progress, with additional testing planned for 2027.

Further certifications for international markets, including the United States and Canada, are in progress, with additional testing planned for 2027.

Beyond fire safety and regulatory compliance, coordinating technical requirements with an ambitious architectural vision is central to delivering high-performing facade systems. The Student Experience International building in Amsterdam, designed by VURB Architects, demonstrates how collaboration between architects and facade specialists, such as SolarLab, can help de-risk technically complex architectural projects during the design and realization of photovoltaic facades. More than 3,000 square meters of individually tilted solar panels cover the building's envelope, with a peak capacity of approximately 283 kWp. Their satin finish and horizontal rotation create changing blue-grey and silver-grey tones as the sun moves, while limiting glare. A limited range of panel formats and a flexible mounting system support durability and maintenance. Integrating energy production into the envelope also leaves the roof available for green areas and shared outdoor activities.

The Student Experience International building in Amsterdam, designed by VURB Architects, demonstrates how collaboration between architects and facade specialists, such as SolarLab, can help de-risk technically complex architectural projects during the design and realization of photovoltaic facades. More than 3,000 square meters of individually tilted solar panels cover the building's envelope, with a peak capacity of approximately 283 kWp. Their satin finish and horizontal rotation create changing blue-grey and silver-grey tones as the sun moves, while limiting glare. A limited range of panel formats and a flexible mounting system support durability and maintenance. Integrating energy production into the envelope also leaves the roof available for green areas and shared outdoor activities.

Together, the projects show that de-risking BIPV is a collaborative process extending from early feasibility through architectural design, engineering, testing, and installation. Making costs, energy performance, technical constraints, and regulatory requirements visible at each stage gives architects and project partners a clearer basis for evaluating and implementing solar facades.

Zac Affleck 'Deeply Regrets' How His 'Unacceptable' Actions Impacted Jen

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Zac Affleck is offering a public mea culpa to his estranged wife, Jen Affleck, prior to the premiere of her new reality show, The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County.

“Over the past several days, I’ve seen the conversations online surrounding my family, my marriage and my past actions that have unfolded onscreen,” Zac, 30, wrote in a Wednesday, November 11, Instagram Story. “I understand why certain moments have been difficult for people to watch, they’re difficult for me too.”

He added, “As I reflect on this time in my life, I fully acknowledge that my words and actions were unacceptable, and they hurt those I care about the most.”

The Afflecks’ marriage troubles have been a recurring story on The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives over the years and are now set to be featured on its Orange County spinoff, which documented the months leading up to a pregnant Jen, 27, filing for divorce in Utah on November 3. (Jen and Zac already share daughters Nora, 5, and Penny, 1, as well as son Luca, 3.)

“I have taken responsibility for my behavior and I deeply regret how my actions negatively affected Jen throughout our relationship,” Zac noted on Wednesday. “There is no excuse for the moments where I failed to show her the kindness, respect, and compassion she deserves.”

Zac revealed he “did not film” any scenes for Mormon Wives: Orange County season 1 and had yet to see any of the episodes before the Hulu show’s Thursday, November 12, premiere.

“The show was filmed during an especially difficult time for Jen and I, and I will watch for the first time when all you do,” he confirmed to his Instagram followers. “However, no matter how certain events may be portrayed and edited onscreen, one fact remains the same: Jen is the mother of my children and someone I’ve loved and shared a life with for years.”

Ahead of the couple welcoming baby No. 4, Zac said he did not want to “unintentionally” cause his pregnant ex any “stress.” Zac claimed that he’d “acknowledged my mistakes directly” to Jen and is intent now on being “the best coparent for our children.”

“Given the nature of Jen’s career, our relationship dynamics have played out in front of an audience but we have many private conversations daily,” he acknowledged. “Jen and I are constantly working to be on great terms and we share a deep sense of respect and appreciation for one another.”

His statement concluded, “My children may see this online someday and all I want them to know is that their parents love them more than anything, and I am committed to showing up as the best version of myself for our family unit.”

For her part, Jen exclusively confirmed to Us that her pregnancy played a huge role in her decision to end her marriage, insisting that “the same reason why I filed for divorce is the same reason why it was really hard.”

“It was this fourth child. I had to ask myself very hard questions like, ‘Is this the type of relationship I want this child to look up to?’” she told Us. “When I realized it was a no, that’s when I realized that I needed to make a change. You’ll see a lot of that unfold in season [1 of the OC spinoff].”

Jen recognized that filming Mormon Wives “for sure sped up” her decision to move forward in her life without Zac.

“When you’re filming and you’re rewatching your life unfold in front of millions of people, it’s the difference between looking in a mirror,” she noted. “It highlights things.”

Jen went to clarify, “It’s like one year of therapy feels actually like 20 years of therapy because it’s so much more magnified when you’re sharing it in front of the world. I feel like if I hadn’t done reality TV, I would have still made this decision. It just would have been way longer down the road.

The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County premieres on Hulu Thursday.

Opinion: Andoni Iraola has decided what he wants to do with 'very strong' defender

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Liverpool have got a lot of decisions to make before the January transfer window opens up.

The Anfield outfit have had a lot of questions asked about the future of their squad with several areas among their ranks still needing to be upgraded.

They may have spent well over £600 million on new players across the last couple of years but, it's still not been enough.

This has caused them to end up fourth in the Premier League which, if we're being honest, is far from ideal.

However, since the winter transfer window is now edging closer, even more decisions will need to be made about the futures of some Liverpool players with Andoni Iraola already making his mind up about one star.

Liverpool may not want to sign Ronald Araujo after Malo Gusto news

In the summer, four new players were brought to Anfield.

All four of them have had a positive impact on the way things have gone in L4 this term with each of them putting on man of the match performances this term.

Of the lot, Ronald Araujo, who has been described as "very strong" in the past, has probably been the most successful.

He's helped solidify the Reds' back line and, without him this season, Iraola's defence would have been tricky to sort out.

However, since he's only on a season-long loan with an option to buy, nobody knows exactly what Liverpool are going to do about his future.

And, thanks to this latest update from TEAMtalk about Malo Gusto, even more questions have been thrown into the mix.

"Malo Gusto is ready to leave Chelsea in the January transfer window, with the club also open to the possibility of his departure, TEAMtalk understands," they wrote.

"Liverpool also made enquiries about the defender in the summer, while Tottenham Hotspur were among the other Premier League clubs to explore his availability.

"Both could yet emerge as potential destinations if Gusto’s situation at Stamford Bridge continues to develop in the coming weeks and particularly with the Reds open to signing a specialist right-back in 2027 owing to their own well-publicised issues with the position and amid doubts that the Reds will fork out to sign Ronald Araujo on a permanent basis.

"However, if Liverpool do press the button on that deal, they will not have things all their own way, with the interest in Gusto not restricted to England, and with several European clubs also keeping tabs on the Frenchman.

"Atletico Madrid, AC Milan, Como and RB Leipzig are among those to have shown an interest, giving Gusto a number of potential routes away from West London.

"Chelsea are aware of the growing interest and are open to the prospect of a January exit."

Malo Gusto being open to leaving Chelsea could cause Liverpool to re-ignite their interest

Right-back is still a problem for Liverpool.

Nobody knows who the Reds' starting full-back is going to be next term and, with Gusto now open to leaving Chelsea, another spanner has been thrown into the works.

Because, on deadline day of the summer transfer window, the Reds made a last-ditch bid for the Frenchman to try and solve their right-back problems.

Indeed, Conor Bradley is due back in the new year but, after spending so much time on the sidelines and after such a serious injury, it's unclear whether he's going to be able to get back to his best or not.

But, with Gusto now being open to leaving and Liverpool's interest well-documented, it could mark the end of the line for Araujo.

Here's hoping that fans receive some sort of clarity soon.
