The High-Rise Bottleneck: Why EV Charging in Indian Apartments Is an Engineering Puzzle

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Most Western clean-energy reporting takes a very specific picture of home charging for granted. You pull into a private driveway, plug into an attached garage wall-box, and pull cheap power while you sleep.

That reality does not exist for most urban drivers in India.

Data from Kazam and the Alliance for an Energy Efficient Economy (AEEE) shows that roughly 70% to 75% of urban households in major Indian metros live in multi-family apartments and cooperative housing societies. Parking means an open ground level slot or a space two levels down in a concrete basement. When someone here buys an electric car, their biggest hurdle isn’t battery degradation or highway chargers. It’s their own building.

As an electrical engineer in India, I watch this play out every week. Someone buys an EV, assumes they can hire a local wireman to pull power from their flat’s meter to their parking bay, and runs straight into transformer limits, cable physics, and nervous managing committees.

Here is what is actually going on beneath the floorboards.

Indian housing societies buy electricity under a bulk “sanctioned load” from their local utility (DISCOM). The utility sizes the local transformer using an electrical diversity factor the simple assumption that not all 100 flats will run their air conditioners, geysers, and induction stoves at the exact same moment.

A domestic geyser pulls 2 kW for ten minutes and shuts off. A 7.4 kW AC wall-box pulls 32 amps continuously for six to eight hours. When several residents plug in around 8:00 PM, that heavy, unbroken draw lands on the transformer right as domestic air conditioning and kitchen loads hit their evening peak.

Let’s look at the numbers on a 100-flat complex running on a standard 250 kVA transformer:

Here is where the grid cracks: just five or six cars charging at 7.4 kW pull 37 kW to 44 kW. That wipes out the 30 kW buffer completely. If eight cars plug in at once (59.2 kW), the transformer goes straight into thermal overload.

When that happens, the main incomer breaker trips plunging common areas and elevators into darkness or the utility slaps the society with massive Maximum Demand penalties on their next power bill. Getting a utility to upgrade a transformer or approve higher sanctioned load requires significant capital, transformer yard space, and months of red tape.

In a detached house, wiring an EV charger means running 5 or 10 metres of cable from your distribution board to the garage.

In an Indian high-rise, your electricity meter sits in a ground-floor panel room or inside a service shaft on the 14th floor. Your car is parked two floors beneath the earth in Basement 2. Connecting the two is an expensive engineering problem:

Even if an owner is willing to foot the entire wiring bill, they still need a No Objection Certificate (NOC) from their housing society committee or Resident Welfare Association (RWA).

Most RWA committees are volunteer residents without technical backgrounds. When handed a request for high-power continuous charging, their hesitation usually comes down to three worries:

The courts are starting to step in. In Amit Dholakia v. The State of Maharashtra (2025), the Bombay High Court pushed state authorities to issue clear guidelines preventing arbitrary NOC rejections. More recently, in early 2026, the Supreme Court of India issued notice on a Public Interest Litigation challenging arbitrary charger bans across states. While the legal momentum is clearly moving toward protecting a resident’s right to charge, taking a court ruling to a heated Sunday morning society meeting rarely makes for a quick installation.

Treating an electric car like an extra microwave where everyone pulls their own line from their domestic meter does not scale. Societies have to build shared electrical infrastructure.

India’s EV transition is moving fast on two and three wheels. But passenger electric cars won’t reach mass adoption until high-rises solve the overnight charging puzzle.

The vehicles are ready, and buyers want them. But until building codes, dynamic load balancing, and society committees catch up with automotive engineering, the hardest part of driving electric in India will simply be charging at home.

To Honor Veterans, How About We Stop The Oil Wars?

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Today is Veterans Day in the United States. Naturally, across the country, people have spent at least part of the day thinking about veterans they know as well as veterans they don’t and thanking them for their service. Whether you agree with the various wars the United States has gotten into or not, you have to honor and appreciate the human beings who offered to put their lives on the line in order to support their country (or think that they’re doing so, at least). I know several people who are veterans, and they typically sacrificed a lot in their previous military roles.

However, I do have to come back to the rationale for the wars we as a country get into. I’m 44 years old, and it seems like we’ve been involved in wars related to oil and gas for more than half of my life. It’s just a continual process. We get into a war in the Middle East connected to XYZ, but it’s really about oil. After years of over-involvement, destruction, and death, society realizes it’s been a super counterproductive, idiotic war and we pull out. The, after a few years, we begin again and the story repeats.

Well, how about we just stop getting involved in oil wars? How about we stop depending so much on oil supply from foreign nations and global oil markets? How about we respect our countrymen and countrywomen who sign up to serve by not sending them into pointless forever wars in the Middle East to get traumatized, injured, or killed? That would be truly honoring veterans of the past as well as protecting current and future Americans.

The Trump Administration’s Pro-Cancer Agenda

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Under Trump, Americans could face an extraordinary increase in exposure to cancer-causing toxics and radiation.

Over the nearly two years of President Donald Trump’s second term, his administration has taken more than 500 actions that harm public health or the environment. When faced with this onslaught of harm, it can be difficult to see the strategic agenda at play. However, NRDC’s new analysis reveals that the Trump administration is executing a sweeping and long planned—but quiet—government-wide campaign to dismember major programs that are intended to prevent exposure to cancer-causing toxics and radiation. In sum, this administration has reversed course: America’s government is no longer fighting a “War on Cancer” but fighting a “War to Promote Cancer.”

This is not an accident. Much of this campaign can be found throughout the Heritage Foundation’s Project 2025 blueprint and has long been advocated for by industry. But a healthy life should not be treated as an obstacle to somebody else’s profit margin. The freedom to breathe clean air, drink safe water, eat safe food, work without needless exposure to carcinogens, and raise children without avoidable toxic risks should be among the most basic expectations of life in America. Americans should not have to wonder whether the water coming from their taps contains cancer-causing chemicals, whether the air they breathe carries toxic pollution from a nearby industrial facility, or whether going to work means being unnecessarily exposed to carcinogens.

Instead of ensuring these basic freedoms, the Trump administration is creating a system in which polluters are given greater leeway to expose everyone in the nation to cancer-causing toxics and radiation.

Democrats Are at COP31 to Show That the US Has Not Abandoned the Fight Against Climate Change

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The results of the US midterms on November 3 delivered a resounding victory for the Democrats, and a major loss for US president Donald Trump and the Republican party.

Gaining control of the Senate and the House of Representatives has emboldened the Democrats, giving them a renewed sense of political legitimacy and a mandate to pursue their agenda. But will this shift translate into meaningful changes in US climate change policy?

The Democratic sweep will change the political dynamics around US climate policy and how the country cooperates with the international community on this issue. Although there was no official US presence at the 2025 COP conference, held in Brazil, a coalition of 100 local US leaders, including governors, mayors and other top city and state officials, attended as part of the US Climate Alliance. Former US vice-president Al Gore, and former US secretary of state John Kerry are part of an unofficial US group at the 2026 gathering.

Kerry said in a speech there: “Americans are committed to taking back their future, and we will build and rebuild our partnerships as we do that.”

Being on the ground at COP is important. As the US has a decisive role to play in global climate policy, sending a non-official coalition signals that many Americans are still working to combat climate change.

Trump has repeatedly stated that climate change is a hoax and refused to attend the COP31 climate talks being held in Turkey. But 20 Democratic members of Congress will be attending, although they have been denied official state department funding for the trip.

During the first and second Trump administrations, the US has been an outlier among wealthy countries when it comes to fighting climate change. Trump has actively backed the use of fossil fuels, undermining clean energy projects, and rolled back legislation that stipulated emission standards for vehicles.

Trump also weakened greenhouse gas reporting requirements and methane regulation. Then in February 2026, the Trump administration rescinded the Environmental Protection Agency’s 2009 finding that greenhouse gases threaten the health and welfare of the public, which had regulated the emissions from new cars and other vehicles. This had provided the legal foundation for regulation under the Clean Air Act.

On the international front, Trump was equally determined to undermine global efforts to fight climate change. Famously, he withdrew from the Paris agreement, part of a global initiative to address climate change, and refused to attend COP summits. But the Democrats’ seismic midterm win may be a sign of optimism for those fighting to address climate change.

The biggest issues of the midterms were affordability, corruption and the Iran war, but issues related to fighting climate change were also on voters’ list. There is growing concern about data centres. As many as 56% of US voters felt that data centres and AI were either a major or even crucial factor in influencing who they voted for, with only 13% saying it was not a factor at all.

Data centres require millions of gallons of water for cooling systems, and this is expected to rise to 73 billion litres of water a year by 2028. Over half of planned AI facilities in the US will be situated in areas that have significant water shortages or drought conditions.

After the midterms, Zoe Lofgren, a Democrat, is likely to become the chair of the House of Representatives’ committee on science, space and technology once the new session starts in January. She wants to tackle the administration’s “climate denialism”.

Lofgren’s leadership, along with new Democratic members of the committee, may offer it more power to reverse some of the Trump administration’s changes to science policy.

However, the Democrat midterm wins will not mean that the US is automatically back at the negotiating table at COP nor that the US will be able to rejoin the Paris Accords while Trump is president. Trump still has the power to make these decisions and can continue to keep the US out of international climate initiatives, regardless of who controls Congress. The US Constitution says nothing about withdrawing from treaties, which creates uncertainty for what the Senate’s role might be.

The election, however, does signal that there is considerable domestic opposition to his approach. And while Congress has not passed follow-up legislation prohibiting presidential withdrawal from the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), it has continued to fund several UNFCCC initiatives. During the Biden administration, the US significantly increased climate finance from US$1.5 billion (£1.1 billion) a year in 2021 to US$9.5 billion in 2023.

Democrats could also push back on Trump’s current policies, using their power to hold hearings, investigate the administration’s dismantling of climate programmes and challenge cuts to scientific research. The Democrats can also use the power of Congress’s control of spending to protect or restore funding to some of these environmental initiatives.

They could also try to introduce legislation that reinstates some of the clean energy initiatives that had been implemented in past administrations to strengthen emissions standards and support clean energy. The big issue, however, is that Trump has made it clear that he is going to be very active in using his veto power, and without a two-thirds majority in Congress, the Democrats will not be able to override this.

What this all means is that the world cannot expect the US to be a reliable and constructive partner at COP with Trump still in power. Trump will probably continue to forge ahead with expanding fossil fuel production, while weakening international climate cooperation, even if the electorate disapproves of these policies.

Ironically, Trump’s greatest contribution to the fight against climate change was his initiation of the war with Iran, which helped to cut global fossil fuel carbon dioxide emissions by 0.4% due the closure of the Strait of Hormuz. But climate scientists note this is not a long-term solution.

While the Democrats’ electoral victory offers a glimmer of hope for renewed climate action, both domestically and internationally, restoring US climate leadership will take time, especially while Trump remains in power.

By Natasha Lindstaedt, Professor in the Department of Government, University of Essex

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The war means Mantis’ Gulf properties are serving only a domestic market for now. Its long-term bet on the region, as it builds out its "circuit" tourism offer, depends on international travelers coming back.

Mantis Collection, half owned by Accor since 2018, is pressing ahead with its Gulf expansion plan despite the U.S.-Iran conflict, which has reshaped demand across its  young portfolio.

Its Bahrain resort shut completely at times during the war’s peak, then recovered quickly, while a new Saudi mountain resort is trading in line with expectations on domestic demand. Two upcoming openings remain on track despite procurement setbacks.

“It would be naive to say the U.S.-Iran war hasn’t had an impact,” said Craig Erasmus, CEO of Mantis Collection. “Although the trends are positive, you need stability to return for a more diverse guest mix [to come back]. The region is very focused on the domestic market now.”

Mantis Collection, the experiential resort brand 50% owned by Accor since 2018, is sticking to its Gulf expansion plan even as the U.S.-Iran conflict narrows its guest mix. Its new Gamra Mountain Resort in Saudi Arabia's Al Baha is trading at around 30% occupancy three months in, all of it domestic, and the conflict has made it harder to build the winter low season. The strategy copies what Mantis built across 17 African properties — multi-property circuits plus an in-house tour operator — with three more committed Saudi properties, a Saudi tour operator launch next year, and a new UAE emirate project to be announced. Mantis is avoiding Dubai and Abu Dhabi entirely.

Kitchen of the Week: High/Low in a Downtown NY Remodel by Loose Parts

Earlier this year, Margot wrote about the Upstate New York kitchen of Jennifer June, founder of Loose Parts and the Workshop Kitchen, a modular system that treats cabinets like freestanding furniture. (You can read the story here.) Today, we’re sharing the every-inch-considered Lower East Side kitchen that Jennifer designed for clients Darrel and Jeff, a psychiatrist and an adventure travel director who split their time between New York City and Upstate.

“The apartment is located in the Seward Park Cooperative, built in 1950 for union housing,” says Jennifer. “The previous owners had worked with Bench Architecture to transform it into a loft-like space by adding new white oak floors and exposing the concrete. Darrel and Jeff were drawn to the light, views, and vision of David Bench. They decided to extend the original vision with a second phase of renovation that included the kitchen (as well as remodeling the bath, adding a second bath and building closets.”

That’s where Jennifer and her waste-conscious design ethos came in. Instead of  ripping everything out and installing a whole new kitchen, she incorporated existing kitchen pieces (including some hardwearing Ikea products) into the redesign. “We shared an appreciation for reusing what was already there,” she says.

Below, she gives us a tour of the couple’s semi-new, completely reimagined space.

xStocks owns 75% of Solana’s tokenized stocks – Rises to $607M in a year

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

xStocks has started listing stocks from outside the US! They dropped 200+ UK and European stocks on Solana [SOL] this week.

xStocks owns most of Solana’s tokenized stock market

But why is this big news? Per data from Blockworks, the supply of tokenized equities on Solana is at $607 million. They report 2,102 products and close to 1.89 million holders.

Back in June 2025, that number was basically zilch. At the beginning of the year, we were at $200 million; $400 million around April. Sometime this fall, we hit $600 million!

The October peak was $620 million, but we’ve fallen down since. Honestly, though? It’s mostly just kept going up.

Most of that has to do with xStocks; their tokens are 75% of the supply. Ondo, Backpack, and Superstate each have a single-digit share; smaller issuers such as PreStocks, Tessera Labs, Remora, and Securitize put up whatever’s left.

Those shares haven’t moved an inch since August, other than a short dip for xStocks a few days ago. There’s a ton of competition to put traditional stocks on Solana, but it’s xStocks’ race to lose.

But remember: Holders are counted by address, and some people use more than one. The real number of investors could be lower.

Blockworks showed about 400,000 holder addresses in early September. Today, it’s closer to 1.9 million.

Daily senders’ best day was at 320K, and the count has gone past 200K on several other days since.

Avalanche is set to settle traders for the NYSE-linked tokenized stock platform. Robinhood is building its own chain on Arbitrum. The DTCC’s tokenization service uses Canton for part of its setup, and word on the street is that Stellar is prepping for an RWA push too!

Everyone wants a piece of the Wall Street pie. Right now, that’s on Solana’s plate. It could be someone else tomorrow.

How Founders Can Reduce Platform Risk Before It Threatens Growth

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

One day, I woke up to a notice that our app had been removed from Apple’s App Store. For the next month or so, we racked our brains trying to find out why. We even reached out to our internal contacts, only for them to tell us that helping us could put their jobs at risk. Nothing seemed to work.

For years, the App Store’s unwritten contract seemed simple. As long as you followed the guidelines, your storefront would remain available. Then a removal notice arrives, and you wonder whether your business can survive.

In 2025, Apple removed 166,899 apps and reported just 423 restorations after appeals. As we learned, being caught in it feels like watching your business collapse, with no guarantee of recovery.

It’s not all bad news, though. We survived, and this episode taught me that platform dependency is ultimately infrastructure risk. As founders, we prepare for cloud outages and payment failures, but losing access to the platform that brings us customers deserves the same attention. Fortunately, we were prepared, and this is how you can be, too.

Financially, Apple immediately freezes funds from the previous three months and turns off recurring subscriptions. For a subscription-dependent company, this can send its monthly recurring revenue to zero.

Before spending another cent on acquisition, consider what would happen to those customers if your app disappeared tomorrow. Could they still use your product and continue paying you?

If the answer is no, every new customer makes your business more dependent on a platform decision you cannot control.

Cal AI, reportedly generating $100,000 daily, was pulled without warning and later reappeared. But its reinstatement tells you little about how long your business might wait.

In Intch’s case, the explanation was so generic it told us nothing. We had 30 days to appeal, but the process ended with a canned rejection.

Your appeal may succeed, but your business must operate while you wait. Preparing in advance lets you keep going, whatever the outcome.

Our experience was grueling, but fortunately, two years earlier we had decoupled from the App Store by building a web version and making it our primary payment channel.

It also let us ship updates 10 times a day while escaping Apple’s predatory commissions. We weren’t the only ones. Pavel Durov, founder of Telegram, has also promoted web access to bypass store restrictions and potential censorship.

The lesson is straightforward. Customers need a working alternative before access disappears. Building one only after removal leaves the business exposed while the team races to replace its storefront.

We initially feared the user experience would suffer, but that concern proved unfounded. After the removal, every key metric fully recovered.

If you share this concern, test it while your app is still available to see whether customers can use and pay for the web version before they must rely on it.

Most founders see the App Store as a distribution channel, but rarely account for how much control it gives Apple over their business.

Before investing more in growth, examine how much revenue depends on continued access and what would survive a removal. Know how long the company could survive an unanswered appeal.

Investors should ask those questions too, because a growing subscription base deserves closer scrutiny when a single platform decision can interrupt its revenue. That exposure belongs in funding and valuation discussions.

Monopolies act in their interests, and if your business falls outside their vision, you may have little say in a decision that could destroy it.

You cannot control it, but you can decide whether it has the power to end your company. By building an alternative while your app is still available, you’ve made the wisest choice.

Yakov Filippenko is a seasoned entrepreneur with more than 10 years of experience in IT and technologies, as well as scaling businesses internationally. As a product manager at Yandex, he led a team that grew the product’s user base from 500,000 to 1.2 million and secured its entry into the international market. Subsequently, he co-founded SailPlay, which he scaled to 45 countries and eventually exited, after it was acquired by Retail Rocket in 2018. In 2021, Filippenko launched Intch, an AI-powered platform connecting part-time professionals with flexible roles.

VCs Are Still Chasing Protein, Even As Fake Meat Falls Out Of Favor

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Macronutrients don’t have popularity contests. But if they did, protein would surely be this era’s winner.

For everything from sodas to snack bars to pasta, it’s now standard practice for food and drink makers to play up protein content whenever feasible. While we may cut carbs and fret over fat, most of us are still keen on protein.

Startups and their backers have been riding this trend too. Over the past decade, investors have poured billions into upstarts promising cheaper, tastier and more sustainable ways to meet our protein quotas.

Unfortunately, many of their earlier bets have fared poorly. Vast sums poured into lab-grown meat several years ago, for instance, have so far failed to produce a success story. Former high-flying plant-based meat startups Beyond (now public) and Impossible Foods have seen their valuations wither. Others have shuttered.

In response, more recent startup investment has shifted away from meat substitutes and toward stuffing more protein into other packaged foods and drinks. It’s a fad that’s obvious on supermarket shelves, where startups and established brands alike relentlessly tout the added protein in their noodles, pancakes, cereals, energy bars, popcorn and so on.

In startup circles, protein-related investment looks tiny in comparison to the billions bet on AI. Deal flow, however, remains fairly robust, with Crunchbase identifying a few dozen protein-focused startups funded in the past year. Moreover, some of these rounds are quite sizable, like a $250 million September Series B for Medici Brands, maker of David Protein snack bars.

To illustrate, we used Crunchbase data to put together sample sets of recently-funded protein-focused companies across several themes. These include packaged foods, drinks, ingredients and processes, and animal feed.

Snacks and packaged foods look like the largest fundraisers among protein startups, securing hundreds of millions in the past year.

For a sense of who’s raising, we put together a list of seven recently funded brands that are finding their way to store shelves.

The bulk of recent funding went to the aforementioned parent of David Protein. That brand, in addition to churning sweet-flavored bars with a protein content to rival cod, has been expanding into ice cream and milkshakes.

Wilde Brands, which makes crackers from chicken breasts, has also proven popular with investors, pulling in $40 million last month. Among early-stage startups, meanwhile, Frozen One has been off to a brisk start, raising over $8 million for a line of high-protein ice creams now selling at Target.

Startups and investors are also catering to people who want their high-protein diet in drinkable form. To illustrate, we put together six recently funded examples below.

Ripple Foods, which makes a vegan milk using pea protein, is the highest historical funding recipient, raising over $300 million in its 12-year-history, even though it hasn’t officially joined the unicorn club.

Clear protein drinks are also increasingly a thing, with multiple startups including protein soda brand Juuz and sports drink purveyor Protein2o.

Quite a few funded startups are focused more on novel, protein-rich ingredients rather than their own branded retail lineup. In many cases, they’re using precision fermentation to deliver.

The Every Co., which makes animal-free egg white proteins through fermentation, is the biggest funding recipient and one of the older names on our list. It’s raised nearly $300 million in the past 10 years, including a $55 million round a year ago.

Early stage is also seeing some action from startups including Edonia, which makes a high-protein cooking ingredient from spirulina,

A number of funded protein startups are also focusing on pets and livestock — a target audience that’s not quite so overwhelmed by supermarket options.

To illustrate, we aggregated five such companies that raised funding in recent quarters.

Notably, two of them — InnovaFeed and nextProtein — are bug-focused. InnovaFeed has raised over $500 million to raise insects for feeding livestock, pet food and aquaculture. NextProtein is using fly larvae for feedstock.

Others are focused on pets. Meatly, for instance, secured a Series A round this spring to build what it says will be Europe’s largest cultivated meat facility. It plans to partner with pet food brands and sell its own branded lineup once production scales.

If we were to encapsulate a broad trend across most of the protein-related financings it would be that startups aren’t trying to convince people that vegetables are meat. Rather, they’re focused largely on protein as an in-demand macronutrient that can be increasingly concentrated and stuffed into a bewildering variety of flavors and form factors.

That might not all taste as good as their more sugary, high-fat and carb-laden alternatives, but at least they’ve got protein.

10 Things Nobody Tells You About Narcissus

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

All Gardenista stories—from garden tours and expert advice to hand tools and furniture roundups.

Starting from scratch or upgrading an outdoor space? Look here for advice on plants and hardscape materials. We have 200 guides on everything from fences to foxgloves.

Heading somewhere? Plan your trip with our destination guides to our favorite public gardens, hotels, restaurants, and shops.

Gardenista’s members-only directory of landscape architects and garden designers.

See our obsessively curated catalog of favorite products sourced by the Gardenista editors.

Nobody ever says “I should have planted fewer narcissus bulbs” when the flowers start blooming in springtime. With their cheery bobbing heads, these happy harbingers of spring lift your spirits when nothing else in the garden has yet dared to declare victory over winter.

Narcissus—or do you say daffodil?—is one of the most familiar flowers in any garden or supermarket. I defy you to find one person who can’t identify a yellow narcissus (or even a white one). And yet there’s so much more to know. We could spend a week on jonquils alone! Let’s get started, with 10 things nobody tells you about Narcissus:

Daffodil is the common name of Narcissus (a Latin word), and therein lies the daffodil’s image problem. It sounds more highfalutin to invoke Latin. When snobs say they don’t like daffodils, what they may be trying to say is they don’t care for the looks of the big, yellow, common varieties of Narcissus sold in supermarkets. That unloved trumpet flower, by the way, is Narcissus pseudonarcissus.

Read more on this topic in Order the World’s Best Narcissi Online: But Please, Don’t Call Them Daffodils.

Jonquils are also narcissi, in case you were wondering. Varieties of the species is Narcissus jonquilla are known for their small, delicate flowers and intense fragrance.

Narcissus secretes a sap—visible when you cut the stems—that can be poisonous to other flowers. Wait at least 24 hours before putting cut flowers into a vase with other varieties. But for best results, keep daffodils segregated in their own vase.

Plant daffodils, tulips, hyacinths, and crocuses together at the edge of a path or walkway. As the flowers bloom in succession, they’ll mask each other’s dying foliage. Because you’re not cutting the stems of Narcissus, they won’t excrete sap to hurt other varieties planted nearby. Bonus: daffodil bulbs repel squirrels and can exert a protective effect on other nearby plants.

Clusters of uneven numbers of flowers look natural in a garden. Even numbers or straight rows can look skimpy or too symmetrical. Read more in Expert Advice from Old House Gardens: 10 Ideas for Planning a Spring Bulb Garden.

Paperwhite bulbs sold for forcing already have been exposed to cool temperatures for a few weeks to prime them to bloom. They carry all their nutrition in their bulbs; all you need to do is put them in a vase or bowl with a little water to encourage their roots to grow. Don’t drown them (extra points if you put a 1-inch layer of gravel or pebbles in the bottom of the vase).

Read more in DIY: Bottle-Fed Paperwhites.

As paperwhites grow, they can get leggy (and floppy) unless you take a simple step to prevent the behavior: Get them drunk. Adding alcohol (ethanol) to the bulbs’ water will stunt the growth of stems (by about one-third of their normal height) but not the blooms of paperwhites.  Read more in DIY: Get Your Paperwhites Drunk for Better Blooms.

Narcissus bulbs are like bears: they go dormant in winter. They need weeks or months of cold temperatures to prepare them to grow. When spring sunlight starts to warm them, they’ll emerge from their sleep.

Read more in Foolproof Spring Bulbs for a Beginner.

The flat side of the bulb is where the roots sprout. The point side sends up leaves.

Hardy perennials that come back year after year (and even spread—or naturalize—if they’re happy), daffodils are one of the easiest bulbs for a beginner to grow. Happy in a wide range of climates (they grow everywhere in the US except southern Florida), they will greet you with cheery yellow faces in springtime.

N.B.: This post is an update; it was first published January 2019.

America Can Do Good—and Do Well

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ANDREW NATSIOS was Administrator of the U.S. Agency for International Development and the President’s Special Envoy to Sudan during the George W. Bush administration. He is the author of Guns Are Not Enough: Foreign Aid in the National Interest, from which this essay is adapted.

Among all the ruinous consequences of the so-called U.S. Department of Government Efficiency’s efforts to cut bureaucracy, none has been as profound as the destruction of the United States’ ability to deliver foreign aid. A comprehensive Lancet study shows that the U.S. Agency for International Development’s assistance prevented more than 90 million deaths between 2001 and 2021. Using a mortality model, Boston University epidemiologist Brooke Nichols has estimated that the 2025 dismantling of USAID contributed to more than 700,000 excess deaths over roughly a year. A study published in Science this May found that after USAID’s shutdown, places that had lost large amounts of the agency’s assistance experienced a marked uptick in armed clashes and riots. The development economists Marcella Nicolini and Fabio Sabatini, meanwhile, showed that economic activity in areas that formerly enjoyed substantial U.S. aid had dramatically declined.

No country can be a great power without a functioning foreign aid program. But today, the United States does not have one. After 90 percent of USAID’s programs were terminated and 90 percent of its staff members were laid off, Congress formally reappropriated most of the budgetary funding dedicated to U.S. foreign aid. But the Office of Management and Budget (OMB) has not released all of this appropriated funding. More damaging still, USAID’s mechanisms for spending aid money have been dismantled. According to the Pew Research Center, annual U.S. foreign aid disbursements fell by $26 billion between the 2023–24 and 2024–25 fiscal years; $2.5 billion in appropriated funding designated for foreign aid went unspent by the end of the 2025–26 fiscal year.

Russell Vought, OMB’s director and a self-identified Christian nationalist, has stonewalled spending for programs related to supporting democracy and insisted that humanitarian grants can be sent only to two specific Christian nongovernmental organizations. U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio, meanwhile, announced that he wanted to end funding to USAID development contractors, universities, and local civil society organizations without understanding the downside of other options for aid dollars, such as budget support, which are far less accountable, uniquely American, and results-oriented.

Given the suffering created by the end of USAID, it is even more troubling that DOGE’s objectives to slash waste and boost transparency have not been realized. U.S. President Donald Trump’s 2025 “big beautiful bill” erased any modest savings won by DOGE’s cuts to foreign aid. And the decision to move USAID’s functions to the State Department, which has weak business systems and inadequate staff to manage aid programs, has thus far led to the transfer of $3.8 billion in U.S. foreign aid money to the UN Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs and $15.9 billion directly into foreign governments’ treasuries through memorandums of understanding.

These governments often have limited capacity to spend the money wisely and produce results. In the past, USAID’s Office of the Inspector General has harshly criticized such options because it is nearly impossible to audit the transfers. Over 90 percent of the countries involved in these agreements score below 50 (out of 100) on Transparency International’s Corruption Perceptions Index, indicating serious systemic corruption. This may explain why State Department political appointees have kept the texts of these health agreements secret.

Clashing demands—that aid should relieve misery abroad, that it ought to serve U.S. military and diplomatic interests, and that it ought to yield rapid, visible, and quantifiable results—always complicated the enterprise. And over the course of 65 years since USAID’s 1961 founding, overregulation, a centralization of control over programs, and congressional meddling created a rigid straitjacket that made the United States less responsive to local governments and local needs. But what the current U.S. administration didn’t understand was that USAID’s bureaucracy, so often presented as an obstacle to effectiveness, was in fact an adaptation to manage these conflicts.

Simply abolishing that institutional architecture with no plan to replace it has only generated more confusion and waste. Meanwhile, China is racing to offer alternative sources of development financing, and Russia is seeking to expand its global influence through security partnerships. The United States cannot afford to let its foreign aid apparatus atrophy. It must redirect it, not reject aid.

Ever since USAID was created, the expectations that leaders in Washington had for foreign aid have conflicted with realities in the developing world, the way actual aid programs are managed, and the speed and nature of societal change. When I read through USAID’s 1960s-era archives to write my new book, I was struck that the demands early U.S. policymakers made of the institution were the same as those made today: policymakers wanted rapid, visible, and quantifiable results. Those demands were as unrealistic then as they are now. U.S. President John F. Kennedy hoped that poverty in Latin America would visibly recede within a couple of years of USAID’s creation, as he was understandably worried that the Cuban revolutionary leader Fidel Castro’s Marxist ideology was spreading across the region. Fourteen months after the organization’s inception, an article by Joseph Kraft in Harper’s Magazine reported that policymakers in Washington had already judged it a failure because it was not delivering “quick and visible results.” David Bell, USAID’s second administrator, recalled in 1965 that Kennedy had complained that the agency was “slow-moving, inefficient, and unresponsive to the President’s policy.”

But diplomacy and development operate on profoundly different time horizons. Nearly all foreign aid programs have diplomatic consequences, even if they aren’t designed to. When diplomatic goals become the main or only goals of an aid program, however—for instance, when U.S. aid kept the Congolese dictator Mobutu Sese Seko in power during the Cold War—good development often fails.

The United States began institutionalizing foreign aid after World War II through agencies and programs such as the Marshall Plan, which supported the postwar economic recovery of Western Europe, and the Point Four Program, a Truman administration initiative to promote development in poor countries to reduce their susceptibility to communism. All along, policymakers debated whether U.S. assistance programs should principally aim to save lives and reduce extreme poverty or to support U.S. foreign policy by countering the influence of hostile powers.

In fact, USAID ended up playing a key role in winning the Cold War. American assistance strengthened governments targeted for Soviet and Chinese communist subversion, particularly in Asia. After U.S. President Richard Nixon entered his second year in office, aid spending for agriculture was increased to 50 percent of USAID’s budget and was directed primarily toward Asia. According to multiple studies by development economists, the effort saved more than 245 million lives. The green revolution, implemented over decades in Pakistan, India, Indonesia, the Philippines, South Korea, and Taiwan (among other countries), helped produce large grain surpluses that fueled industrialization and economic growth and averted the kind of famines that historically produce coups or mass uprisings.

Throughout this era, however, Washington’s demands that aid accomplish diplomatic goals—and quickly and visibly—also undermined the most important elements of development programs: institution building and policy reform. Strong institutions separate wealthy countries from poor ones. The former have them in abundance, while the latter do not. Institution building and policy reform should be the focus of aid programs. But institution building and policy reform is difficult to measure, takes many years to implement successfully, and is not immediately visible. Instead, policymakers insisted that USAID do service delivery, producing valuable humanitarian programs that were ultimately unsustainable.

Most critical analyses of U.S. foreign aid conclude that USAID came to focus on process over substance, resulting in excessive bureaucracy. What these critiques overlook is what caused this obsessive focus on process. A web of federal regulatory and oversight agencies (what scholars call the counterbureaucracy) loosed on U.S. aid programs an army of federal auditors, accountants, regulators, compliance officers, and congressional staffers dedicated to reining in the bureaucracy and—supposedly—protecting taxpayers’ money. This onslaught slowed down USAID’s implementation of programs and created massive amounts of paperwork. It did not make aid programs more effective.

Historically, Congress ended up playing a particularly intrusive role in the management of U.S. foreign aid programs. USAID reported to 14 congressional committees, which often clashed, pulling USAID in different directions and slowing program implementation. (For example, the House and Senate agriculture committees, under the influence of the agriculture lobby, stonewalled efforts to reform food aid by insisting it be exported from the United States, while legislative appropriators and authorizers from foreign affairs committees strongly supported local purchase reform.) Congress also imposed intrusive earmarks—defined amounts allocated for specific programs, projects, or organizations. When countries asked for specific help, USAID often could not respond, because the glut of earmarks meant there was no discretionary money left.

Starting in the 1990s, the State Department attempted to exert more control over the USAID budget. A 1998 statute, for instance, made the USAID administrator report directly to the secretary of state. In January 2001, the State Department took control of USAID’s budget. That only led lawmakers—who often distrusted State—to add more earmarks. To protect U.S. foreign aid programs from an especially hostile Senate Foreign Relations Committee chairman, Republican Senator Jesse Helms, the Clinton administration instructed each cabinet-level department to create its own aid programs that would fall outside that committee’s jurisdiction. For example, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention at the Department of Health and Human Services established regional offices around the world, the Environmental Protection Agency developed solar energy projects in poor countries, and the Federal Emergency Management Agency signed agreements with more than 28 developing countries to assist in disaster management. USAID was already running programs such as these, yielding overlapping mandates which added to the incoherence of U.S. foreign aid.

When I became USAID administrator in 2001, I discovered a foreign aid program whose funds were straitjacketed by nearly 250 congressional earmarks, a sum exceeding USAID’s total appropriation. At the time, Ethiopia was one of the most severely food insecure countries in the world. Yet in 2007 only 1.5 percent of USAID’s budget was devoted to agricultural development, 1.5 percent to economic growth, and 1.5 percent to governance reform; Congressional earmarks made it impossible to fund these programs to match the Ethiopian government’s request.

Despite all these impediments, foreign aid remained a critical component of U.S. foreign policy. The Department of Defense had a particularly productive partnership with USAID, recognizing that foreign aid could be as important as force in incentivizing insurgents to make peace. The effects of USAID projects in Afghanistan were profound but took shape over long time horizons neither the State Department nor the Defense Department had any patience for. (USAID’s Afghanistan media-development program, for instance, was a great success, as documented in a Harvard Kennedy School study by The New York Times’s David Rohde.) Major academic studies have shown that in Iraq, community development projects undertaken by USAID’s Office of Transition Initiatives (OTI) employed large numbers of young men who might otherwise have been recruited into militias, reducing U.S. military casualties in the provinces with the highest levels of violence. In 2004, Army General Peter Chiarelli called the USAID officer managing the program, Kirk Day, a “hero” for saving his soldiers’ lives.

The DOGE team abolished OTI last year, along with innumerable other programs that alleviated suffering globally and enhanced U.S. soft power. When Congress restored many cuts to foreign aid, it did not reinstate USAID’s veteran staff, historical archives, business systems, field presence, or implementing partner networks of universities, international nongovernmental organizations, local civil society groups, and international development contractors. The transfer of programs to the Department of State only exacerbated the contradictions that have long bedeviled U.S foreign aid. Few secretaries of state (or secretaries of defense) have any development background, experience, or technical knowledge, and paradoxically, when aid is seen simply as a tool of diplomacy or defense rather than an end in itself, the diplomatic good it can do is compromised.

Dismantling USAID did not abolish the need for technically knowledgeable staff and bureaucratic systems. In May 2025, the State Department inspector general issued an intimidating report detailing what the department will have to do to competently manage foreign aid programs, a list of demands it will have difficulty meeting. And in September this year, the Government Accountability Office issued a highly critical audit of the State Department’s inadequate management of foreign aid programs, finding that the department lacked documented standards for deciding which aid programs to terminate, could not produce data on its own staffing, and maintained incomplete or incorrect records on grants and agreements. This result was unsurprising, given that the State Department is a diplomatic policy institution, not a program management agency. Merging USAID into the State Department makes as much sense as merging Microsoft with ExxonMobil, given the disparate time horizons on which the two institutions operate and their differences in mission statements and business systems.

The State Department has pursued alternative arrangements for distributing aid that require less direct management. For example, it has negotiated 36 memorandums of understanding with governments in developing countries to transfer U.S. aid funds to their health ministries, so they can spend the program money themselves. But in return for these transfers, Washington has asked these countries to send private health-care data to the U.S. government, accept undocumented immigrants the U.S. government deports, and, in some cases, give U.S. mining companies access to their rare-earth minerals. Because health programs save lives, they should be distributed based on need, not on parochial, transactional American demands.

Many countries have understandably resisted this kind of bargain. USAID had technical staff that could assess a foreign government’s ability to manage funds responsibly. In practice, it ran a detailed and thorough vetting of nearly every aspect of the governments it partnered with. The State Department, by contrast, only vets according to the legal minimum and treats foreign governments as it treats as international or U.S.-based NGOs. State’s approach will increase the risk that funds are stolen by local government ministries that lack the control systems needed to ensure accountability.

The very public and grossly inaccurate attacks on USAID’s integrity and management by Trump and DOGE’s erstwhile leader, Elon Musk, have damaged the agency’s reputation so extensively that simply resurrecting it will be difficult. But Washington’s aid apparatus also needs more coherent organization than it was afforded in the past. In his celebrated work Bureaucracy, on which my book is structured, the political scientist James Q. Wilson argues that strong U.S. departments or agencies need “relative autonomy”: clearly defined missions they do not substantially share with other agencies. By this logic, if the United States wants a high-functioning, coherent foreign aid program, it must first consolidate all aid functions within one department.

Abolishing field offices (called USAID missions) also made administering aid programs much more difficult, blunted true accountability and U.S. influence in the developing world, and reduced the visibility of U.S. aid programs. These country missions must be reestablished, reporting to the local U.S. ambassador. In Washington, Congress should create a new cabinet-level department reporting to the president that manages all the government’s aid programs, which would make it possible to trim staff, streamline program implementation, and avoid policy incoherence. At least 80 percent of this new department’s staff should be deployed abroad, not in Washington. The United States will always need to be able to launch aid programs with an expressly diplomatic purpose and short time horizon, and the State Department should determine these projects and their spending levels. But it should leave the programs’ management up to the new aid department—as it did during the Cold War, when USAID was independent of State.

This new department should also report to fewer congressional committees—a Foreign Affairs authorizing committee and an appropriations subcommittee in each legislative body. The practice of burdening the United States’ foreign aid budget with domestically appealing earmarks—which prevents aid from responding to foreign countries’ own needs—must be reduced.

In the past, the overregulation and bureaucratization of foreign aid driven by the demands of the counterbureaucracy prioritized process over programs and compliance over developmental outcomes. The new aid department must be given regulatory relief. One way to do that is for Congress to set up charter missions, similar to charter schools, as an experiment to see whether accountability deteriorates. These deregulated charter missions would be free from burdensome reporting requirements the counterbureaucracy demands. In recent years, the World Bank has tried a similar approach and found that it improved its programs’ effectiveness without increasing fraud.

The status quo for U.S. foreign aid cannot last. But Washington cannot revert to the old status quo, which generated the kind of dissatisfactions among policymakers that helped lead to its dismantling. Now is the time to bring more coherence to U.S. foreign aid programs with these reforms, if the new Democratic Congress and future administrations show a forward-looking vision.

Trump Should Refashion the Foreign Aid Agency, Not Dismantle It

The Paradox of Dollar Dominance

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

GORDON LIAO is Chief Economist at Circle.

ESWAR PRASAD is a Professor at Cornell University, a Senior Fellow at the Brookings Institution, and the author of The Doom Loop: Why the World Economic Order Is Spiraling Into Disorder.

TONY ZHANG is an Associate Professor at Arizona State University.

This article is based on a paper the authors prepared for the August 2026 Jackson Hole Economic Policy Symposium.

How Technology Is—and Isn’t—Changing the Global Economy

Gordon Liao, Eswar Prasad, and Tony Zhang

It is no secret that much of the world is eager to escape from the clutches of the dollar-dominated global financial system. For over a century, that system has given the United States enormous financial clout, which it has wielded in applying financial sanctions against its adversaries and, in recent decades, to borrow money to fund its budget and trade deficits. For decades, foreign governments have tried to weaken the dollar’s grip, but they have had little success in establishing their currencies as a legitimate alternative.

Now, a new force has emerged that could shake things up: technology. Financial innovations, especially in digital currencies and electronic payment systems, could finally give other currencies a better chance of competing with the dollar. These innovations are making it easier and cheaper to exchange currencies directly for one another rather than using the dollar as an intermediary currency.

But for all the new opportunities these innovations create for currency exchange, reduced frictions in global transactions might only reinforce the dollar’s dominance. Even if costs decrease for using dollar alternatives, foreign investors will not capitalize on them if those countries’ financial markets remain unsteady and their institutions weak. As U.S. government debt soars, the country’s rule of law erodes, and the Federal Reserve’s independence comes into question, the dollar’s dominance may seem uncertain. But the dollar could remain supreme by default.

Continued dollar dominance could lead to further global economic turmoil, as the United States antagonizes its trading partners and persists with undisciplined policies that result in large budget deficits and trade deficits. There is a chance that the dollar, too, loses the trust of foreign investors—a prospect of comeuppance that would no doubt be received with glee in many capitals. But governments should be careful what they wish for. A world bereft of a reliable currency of last resort at times of global financial panic could be even more unstable than one dominated by Washington.

The U.S. dollar should be losing its dominance in global finance for a host of reasons. First is the country’s penchant for weaponizing its currency. Across administrations, Washington has imposed financial sanctions on rivals, such as Iran and Russia, cutting off their access to dollar transactions and thereby kneecapping their foreign trade. The economies of U.S. allies sometimes get tangled up in these sanctions, too, when the U.S. government threatens to use secondary sanctions to cut off their banks and corporations from access to dollars in order to deter third parties from transacting with the sanctioned countries. In 2014, for instance, U.S. authorities penalized the French bank BNP Paribas for processing dollar payments on behalf of clients in Cuba, Iran, and Sudan. The French government protested the fine to no avail. In light of such heavy-handed behavior, government officials and central bankers outside the United States have hardly been shy about their intentions to reduce their dollar dependence.

Washington’s broader policy choices, especially the lack of fiscal discipline, should also be undermining confidence in American financial markets. U.S. federal government debt now exceeds $40 trillion, nearly 125 percent of the country’s annual GDP, and there are scant signs that Washington is trying to rein it in. Such high debt levels usually lead to some combination of high inflation, soaring interest expenditures, and currency depreciation. The Trump administration’s hostile approach to international trade, including with close allies, is also causing governments to consider “de-risking” their economic ties to the United States. President Donald Trump’s open attacks on the independence of the Federal Reserve and weakening of checks and balances are eroding the pillars of the U.S. institutional system that underlies foreign investors’ trust in the dollar.

And indeed, there are some signs of the dollar’s decline. The currency’s share of foreign exchange reserves held by foreign central banks has fallen from 71 percent in 2000 to 57 percent today. Yields on U.S. Treasury securities—the asset long seen as the ultimate safe haven for investors during financial turmoil—have risen, meaning investors are demanding a higher return for lending to the U.S. government. In October, the yield on ten-year Treasury notes hit 5.3 percent, the highest level since 2002.

In most other dimensions, however, the dollar looks as dominant as ever. Sixty percent of cross-border transactions are denominated in dollars when excluding the eurozone, which is defined by the common use of the euro, and that number has only gone up in recent years. The euro’s share of cross-border payments outside the eurozone now stands at about 13 percent, and the renminbi’s is less than three percent. The dollar remains preeminent in most other measures of a currency’s international usage, too, including its share of trading activity in foreign exchange markets and denomination of international debt securities, which corporations use to raise funds in global capital markets.

In short, no serious rivals to the dollar have emerged. The reason is simple: other major currencies look even shakier than the dollar. Traditional reserve currencies such as the euro, the yen, and the pound sterling have been undermined by their countries’ weak economies and political instability. Although currencies such as the Australian and Canadian dollars, the Swedish krona, and the Swiss franc have filled in some of the gap, each of these is too small to be consequential in global finance. The dollar’s most discussed putative rival, the renminbi, has also faded. Beijing has done little to alleviate the Chinese economy’s deep-rooted problems, including a prolonged property market slump, high levels of local government debt, and tight government control over the economy and financial markets. Moreover, the Chinese government has been unwilling to allow for the free flow of money across its borders or for market forces to determine its currency’s exchange rate. The renminbi has, in turn, failed to garner greater international use. The result is a second tier of currencies that is increasingly fragmented, with none able to pose any real challenge to the reigning currency hegemon.

In the era of modern financial markets, one of the main purposes of an international currency has been to facilitate indirect transactions among different currencies. It has long been easier and cheaper for a Chinese importer of Brazilian iron ore to exchange renminbi for dollars and pay for the ore in dollars and for the Brazilian exporter to then convert those dollars into Brazilian reais. This is because cross-border payments have long been dominated by a dollar-based global banking network, making dollars the most plentiful currency to conduct payments with. But in recent years, digital technologies have created cheaper and quicker pathways for moving money across borders.

One critical innovation has been the tokenization of money and financial assets, which has been facilitated by technology called blockchain. Blockchain, essentially a public digital ledger that is managed by a computer algorithm rather than by a single bank or clearinghouse, offers a platform for tokenized assets to trade. It does not matter which country the parties are located in, as long as they have Internet access. In the last few years, tokenization has expanded to include not just cash but also financial assets such as government bonds, equities, and mutual funds.

In theory, these changes have the potential to reshuffle the international currency order. Over the past few years, countries such as Brazil, China, India, and Russia have been conducting more trade in their own currencies by building their own cross-border payment networks and linking them to one another, in an effort to reduce their reliance on the dollar. The quantity of these transactions is still modest, because the currencies have limited use outside their countries, and it is still easier and cheaper to transact in a well-recognized and widely accepted currency such as the dollar. But as the countries’ payment systems and financial markets develop further, that number could increase dramatically, altering the currency landscape. Blockchain-based financial innovations could add momentum.

Cross-border investment could become much easier, too. Currently, investing in foreign securities involves going through brokers and other middlemen who demand hefty fees and take days to process transactions. But the tokenization of bonds and equities has expanded investors’ access to a broader swath of international financial assets, even in emerging-market and developing economies, where financial markets tend to be small, thinly traded, and not well connected to global trading networks. In the same way, it has expanded the access of entrepreneurs and small businesses to global pools of capital previously out of their reach, which could particularly help businesses in countries with underdeveloped financial markets. Bank deposits are likely to be available in tokenized form soon, too, which will make it easier to conduct transactions through more traditional payment channels.

But the spread of tokenization might strengthen the dollar’s dominance rather than weaken it. The example of stablecoins illustrates this paradox. Whereas cryptocurrencies such as Bitcoin are created and distributed by a computer algorithm, meaning they have nothing anchoring their value, the value of a stablecoin is typically pegged to an official currency. This value is stable because it is backed one to one by stores of safe and liquid assets—usually government bonds, bank deposits, and cash. Those assets are usually denominated in the dollar. More than $250 billion in stablecoins is now in circulation, and over 99 percent of that value is tied to the dollar. As long as the dollar remains preeminent, investors will have little incentive to back stablecoins with any other currency.

Dollar-denominated stablecoins are a boon for the United States. By solidifying the dollar’s central position in international payments, rising demand for dollar-backed stablecoins also indirectly fuels demand for Treasury securities, lowering the yields the U.S. government has to offer investors to buy those securities and making it easier to fund U.S. budget deficits. Other payment innovations could make it even cheaper to use the dollar for cross-border transactions, minimizing the relative advantage that such innovations provide other currencies. Financial innovations built on new technologies cannot by themselves make up for other currencies’ existing weaknesses—namely, the weak economies and institutions that back them up.

There is another major shift underway in the global economy that favors the dollar’s fortunes and that financial innovations are likely only to reinforce. In the first two decades of this century, central banks—particularly those of large emerging-market economies such as China and India—acquired vast troves of foreign assets denominated in hard currencies such as the euro, pound sterling, Japanese yen, and, most of all, the dollar to increase their foreign exchange reserves. These reserves helped those central banks cope with volatility in capital flows and exchange rates, which is a recurring hazard for countries that fall in and out of favor with foreign investors—often because of factors beyond their control, such as changes in U.S. interest rates. But in the last few years, central banks have stopped adding to their reserve stockpiles or have begun diversifying into other assets, such as gold, because they are reluctant to increase their investments in a country with fickle economic and geopolitical policies. In 2010, foreign central banks accounted for 70 percent of U.S. Treasuries held by foreign investors, a share that has dropped to about 40 percent as demand has leveled off. The retreat of these once reliable sources of demand threatens Washington’s ability to borrow cheaply.

Luckily for the United States, there is an alternative force that is more than making up for this loss in demand. As the costs to cross-border asset trade fall because of financial innovations, private investors from both advanced and emerging-market countries are increasing their foreign investments in search of higher returns and to diversify their portfolios. And they are flocking to dollar assets, from Treasuries to corporate bonds to equities, because U.S. financial markets offer an unmatched combination of good returns, low trading costs, and legal protections. In the present decade, net inflows of financial capital into the United States have been positive for practically every quarter. Despite chatter about de-risking and diversification since Trump’s election, these inflows have only ramped up in recent years.

Greater financial innovation will intensify rather than reverse these trends, delivering yet another blow to countries seeking to strengthen their currencies and counter dollar weaponization. These trends may hurt small and institutionally weak countries most of all. Those around the world who have long trusted the dollar more than their own currencies now have easier access to it—and they are likely to use it. Increased use of dollar-backed payment systems and investments in dollar assets could cause those countries to cede their monetary sovereignty, weakening their central banks’ ability to steer domestic interest rates and inflation. This phenomenon of “dollarization” has long been rampant in parts of Latin America, which have suffered repeated bouts of high inflation and which, through trade, tourism, and remittances from emigrants, have ready access to dollar cash. Financial innovation could also spread that phenomenon to other corners of the developing world, concentrating even more of the world’s savings—and its financial risks—in a single currency managed by a single central bank.

In theory, the best, most stable international monetary system would be truly multipolar, with no single currency in a position of dominance but with several major currencies—each backed by deep financial markets, sound economic policies, and trustworthy institutions—anchoring international trade and finance. In the period before World War I, the British pound sterling, the French franc, and the German mark all denominated transactions and served as reserve currencies. When trouble struck, although sporadically, the countries’ central banks supported one another, helping to stave off broader financial panic. Renewed currency competition would force the competing governments, including the United States, to adopt responsible policies to avoid their currencies being shunned by investors. But given the economic and institutional malaise plaguing China, Japan, the United Kingdom, and the eurozone, such an outcome seems unlikely.

If the dollar loses its position of privilege and no single currency is able to replace it, the global currency system will become even more fragmented than it is now. Such a scenario could unfold if U.S. debt grew so large that investors began doubting its value and dumped it, if additional sanctions and tariffs drove countries to shun the dollar despite the costs, or if the Fed’s independence were seriously compromised, leading to a surge in inflation and a plunge in the dollar’s value. As things stand, if the dollar lost its position of privilege, it would be replaced not by a handful of strong currencies but by a number of weak ones, none backed by sufficiently robust financial markets or a central bank able and willing to serve as the world’s lender of last resort during a crisis.

If the global economic system has no currency to anchor global investors’ trust, stress in financial markets could spiral into unstoppable turmoil. During the global financial crisis of 2008–9, for example, the Fed pumped a huge number of dollars into U.S. and international financial markets to prop up banks and corporations around the world that desperately needed dollars to meet their debt obligations and reinforce their balance sheets. Even though the United States had been the epicenter of the crisis, trust in the Fed helped prevent a terrible situation from becoming even worse. Investors who were anxious about the safety of their assets piled into Treasuries, an investment they trusted would be returned.

Greater dollar dominance, then, is the most likely scenario for the future. The danger of this outcome is that the United States will have even less incentive than before to be more fiscally responsible. If foreign investors remain eager to invest in U.S. financial markets, Washington will have little reason to reduce its budget and trade deficits. Fed policies, including interest rate changes, could cause whiplash effects for other countries. In 2013, for example, the mere prospect of the Fed reducing measures to keep U.S. interest rates low sent currencies in Brazil, India, Indonesia, and Turkey tumbling because investors around the world began anticipating higher returns on U.S. assets and pulled their money out of those countries’ stocks and bonds. As capital flows in and out of markets in response to the Fed’s actions rather than in response to their own monetary policy, other countries could be left bereft of policy tools to stabilize their economies and financial markets in such a scenario. Emerging-market and developing economies, which have shallow financial markets and rely heavily on foreign borrowing, often in dollars, are especially vulnerable to volatile capital flows and exchange rates. In a world of greater dollar dominance, those countries could face more frequent currency crises, capital flight, and debt distress.

As disconcerting as the dollar’s continued preeminence might seem, however, the world needs a currency it can trust. As threats to international financial stability accelerate, financial markets can ill afford to be left adrift. One way or another, the world will have to learn to live with currency disorder, and the dollar, at least for now, is the least bad option.

8 Best Holiday Wreaths for a Festive Front Door (2026)

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Nothing gets us feeling warm and fuzzy quite like passing by a fully adorned front door. Whether lit or unlit, fresh or faux, a well-appointed Christmas wreath is a visual signifier to all who pass that the festivities have officially begun in this home. While a classic ring of evergreens will never go out of style, there’s no shortage of modern takes on traditional winter foliage that will earn you points for both holiday spirit and good taste.

If cleaning up pine needles isn’t your thing, fear not: Faux wreaths are getting closer to the real thing by the season, saving you a little maintenance during an already busy time of year. Choose one accented with velvet ribbons for a touch of nostalgia, or opt for something that looks as though it was plucked straight from a whimsical snowscape, complete with pinecones and berries. And if you’ve historically never considered yourself a “wreath person,” take this as your sign to make this the year you become one. Turns out, participating in holiday cheer is just as delightful as it sounds.

While you may turn to Quince for an affordable cashmere sweater or linen bedding, the unsung treasures might surprise you. Take this faux foliage wreath, for instance, which mimics the look and feel of a Norfolk pine so convincingly, you’d never know the difference from the street. Simple and full, its bare branches make the perfect backdrop for trailing red bows, tinsel, or whatever other accoutrements you desire.

This faux greenery features impressively lifelike cypress leaves, while the berries add a bold, seasonal color. It’s designed for indoor and outdoor use and has UV protection. In short, it’s designed with durability in mind to outlast wintry weather and beyond. It'll also keep you from sweeping a single pine needle off your front porch (a gift in itself).

If evergreens, real or fake, aren't your thing, we say go full color. This Lulu and Georgia wreath is made of velvety baubles, each with a plush texture and rich hue. It's the perfect size for hanging from a cabinet, chair back, or interior door when you want to add a simple but effective holiday touch.

Something to make your days merry and bright, literally. This faux wreath comes pre-lit, saving you the hassle of weaving your own lights through the branches (and sparing you any DIY allegations). With softly draping cedar branches and a warm white glow, it makes an inviting addition to front doors, mantels, and more.

There's something especially charming about holiday decorations that look as though they've been gathered on a winter walk. This Magnolia wreath captures that feeling with lifelike foliage and bright red berries that add just the right amount of color. Even better, it’s currently 25% off. Hang it on your front door or prop it on a mantel for a dose of old-fashioned holiday spirit.

A few perfectly placed pinecones can make a wreath feel understated yet exceptionally sophisticated. This Afloral wreath is the kind of arrangement that lets everyone approaching your front door know they’re about to enter a well-decorated home, though you’d never make a big deal of it. The real-touch greenery is convincing enough to have guests wondering when you found the time to forage a Noble Fir yourself.

If you’re craving a departure from the expected, this copper wreath offers a fresh take on traditional holiday foliage. Eucalyptus and assorted leaves are finished in shimmering shades of copper and gold, lending the arrangement a decidedly more glamorous feel. Think less woodland Christmas, more fancy dinner party.

If you’re dreaming of a white Christmas, this wreath will certainly do the trick. Covered in clusters of snowy white berries, it has a soft, wintry look that feels merry without leaning too heavily into traditional red and green. It's a lovely option for anyone who prefers a more pared-back holiday palette, and one that will look just as fitting long after the presents have been unwrapped.
