Who could replace Jensen Huang? A highly unscientific list of possible successors

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

It’s almost inconceivable that Nvidia would fire its rock star CEO. Yet here we are, on the precipice of this happening. Over at Kalshi, Jensen Huang’s odds of surviving the week are sitting at a lowly 30%. Even though he built one of the world’s most valuable — and, many would argue, dangerously powerful — companies, the tides have completely turned against him. He has confessed to an extramarital affair, but as of Wednesday afternoon it isn’t even clear whom he meant: Alice Huang, the Chinese executive suspected by U.S. intelligence of spying for her country, or Kimberly Powell, an Nvidia executive who reports to him and who, five sources told The New York Times in an explosive new report, was also a paramour.

Even if Huang is pushed out as CEO, the board members he has made very rich are unlikely to cut him loose entirely. Barring another shoe dropping, they could keep him on as chairman, a role Nvidia doesn’t currently have. But someone still has to run the company, and there’s no apparent succession plan, which leaves us, like everyone else, to speculate while we wait to see what happens next. While Huang’s 60 direct reports undoubtedly get a closer look, consider this our own parlor game, featuring some obvious and less obvious candidates:

Lisa Su. The AMD CEO runs Nvidia’s most credible rival, as well as a distant cousin of Huang. The Information floated her name this week, but why would she leave her job for his? Her company stands to benefit from every day that Nvidia spends distracted by an investigation, congressional briefings, and all the mini dramas that are sure to follow. Her best move might be to send a nice note and keep her current corner office.

Eric Schmidt. He was the pick of Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, senior associate dean for leadership studies at the Yale School of Management, who we talked with earlier this week. Schmidt could “parachute in tomorrow and do a very good job,” Sonnenfeld said, and perhaps he could. He ran Google from 2001 to 2011, chaired the National Security Commission on AI from 2018 to 2021, and now runs the rocket company Relativity Space. Chips, software, government, rockets — it’s an impressive range. Then again, Schmidt would bring a lot of baggage when it comes to his own extramarital dalliances. It’s hard to see him clearing the board for that reason.

Lip-Bu Tan. Intel’s CEO knows chips, and right now he might welcome a very soft landing. Last month, Intel reported third-quarter revenue up 21% to $16.5 billion, then warned its fourth-quarter results would fall short of Wall Street’s forecasts. The AI boom has caused a memory-chip shortage, pushing up PC prices and cutting PC sales. That’s hurt Intel (PC chips are a big part of its business), and its stock plunged 20% in after-hours trading on the warning, having already slid more than 30% since September. A move to a company that’s eating everyone’s lunch would be a pretty amazing escape hatch.

Bret Taylor. The former Salesforce co-CEO and OpenAI board chair now runs the AI startup Sierra, and his resume includes a lot of the biggest brands in Silicon Valley. He co-created Google Maps, served as Facebook’s CTO, and chaired Twitter’s board through its sale to Elon Musk. He then took over as OpenAI’s board chair after the “Blip,” Sam Altman’s firing and fast return. When companies need an unflappable, likable adult in the room, they call Taylor. He probably knows nearly every customer Nvidia has, too.

Ian Buck. If the board feels strongly about having a “builder” at the helm, they could do worse than the Nvidia VP who helped develop CUDA, the chip software that has become central to Nvidia’s dominance in AI. Analysts and former employees have floated him as a possible successor, and Kalshi traders give him a middling shot. He’s no household name, but the Stanford PhD seems more viable if Huang can can keep that chairman title and push for a successor he can work with.

Someone else down the hall. Andrew Bell, Nvidia’s senior vice president of hardware engineering, arrived 25 years ago as a fresh Cornell grad and has been on an upward trajectory at the company ever since. Like Buck and dozens of other executives who are presumably being scrutinized, he reports directly to Huang. Nvidia CFO Colette Kress is the insider most often named, but analysts say she isn’t necessarily a natural successor as an innovator and visionary, and Bell has the chip pedigree.

The wild card? Lori Huang. Nobody has floated her, but hear us out: Jensen met his wife as lab partners at Oregon State, so she has been working with him since before Nvidia existed. After graduating, she worked at Silicon Graphics as a micro chip engineer (making more money than her husband, he has said), then scaled back to raise the couple’s family, and she now co-leads the family trusts. If the board wants continuity, they could do worse than a humbler Huang who is also a brainiac. There’s a reason they say that behind every great man is a great woman (and vice versa).

floor-to-ceiling bookcases merge storage and architectural identity in modern homes

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

the home library is making a comeback with floor-to-ceiling bookcases that turn ordinary walls into architectural focal points, blending storage, craftsmanship, and personal identity.

Muhammad edited by christina vergopoulou November 12, 2026 2 min

As contemporary interiors increasingly balance multifunctional living spaces with digital media, floor-to-ceiling bookcases are re-emerging as primary architectural features. Historically confined to enclosed rooms defined by dark wood paneling and built-in millwork, the modern home library integrates seamlessly into open-plan living areas, dining spaces, home offices, and transitional corridors. By deploying full-height shelving, ranging from bespoke cabinetry to modular, freestanding systems offered by furniture retailers like Emma Mason, designers utilize vertical wall surfaces to maximize storage capacity without reducing usable floor area.

By extending library-style bookcases’ shelving to the ceiling, furniture retailer Emma Mason creates continuous vertical lines that draw the eye upward, visually increasing perceived ceiling height and anchoring large wall expanses. A recurring functional and formal component in these systems is the rolling library ladder. Originally designed to access upper-tier storage, modern rolling ladders incorporate streamlined metal rails and secure anti-tip hardware, adding kinetic movement and craft detailing to residential configurations while requiring measured floor clearance for safe operation.

Materiality and finish directly inform the spatial character of the library wall. Deep walnut, ebony stains, and dark painted surfaces (such as navy, charcoal, or muted green) create visual depth and provide high-contrast backdrops for colorful book spines and objects. Conversely, light oak, natural woods, and white or cream finishes minimize visual bulk, aligning with minimalist and Scandinavian interior palettes. Structural planning remains critical: designers must evaluate shelf spans and joinery load capacities to prevent bowing, verify diagonal clearance angles to tilt tall freestanding units upright during installation, and incorporate integrated LED lighting strips alongside negative spacing to prevent visual clutter.

Over the last week, Hyperliquid [HYPE] has dropped 17%, trading at $72.49 as of writing. Despite bullish exchange listings on South Korea’s Upbit and Bithumb on the 10th of November, the token has struggled to regain ground after falling below $87-$89. The latest price action suggests that sellers have continued to dictate the direction of the market.

At the same time, selling pressure has been rising from several directions. On the 31st of October, two large HYPE holders sold $109 million in tokens after Intercontinental Exchange (ICE) released a more relaxed regulatory statement about perpetual Futures.

Then, on the 8th of November, Binance reopened HYPE withdrawals, giving holders access to their tokens again after the exchange’s security breach in October.

HYPE’s weekly chart has remained under pressure over the past seven days, with price breaking below the 50% Fibonacci level and moving into the 61.8% retracement zone. At the time of writing, HYPE was trading around $72.49 and had officially entered the golden pocket.

The indicators also pointed to the market cooling as the weekly RSI stood at 51.07 and continued to trend lower, suggesting that the bullish momentum seen in previous months was beginning to fade.

Furthermore, OBV had declined from its August-September highs, clearly displaying the difficulty buyers had faced in matching the volume behind recent selling.

The daily chart also offered very little evidence of a reversal happening anytime soon. HYPE remained below the 23.6% Fibonacci retracement and is hovering close to its recent swing low at $70.44. An RSI of 35.35 pointed towards continued bearish momentum.

On the other hand, OBV’s recent uptick suggested that buyers’ interest had peaked; however, sustained buying would be needed for price to have any meaningful recovery.

A break below $70.44 could shift attention towards the weekly $69.03 retracement. If buyers defend this area, some relief could be seen, potentially giving HYPE room to attempt a recovery. However, if this support gives way, the next downside target sits at the weekly Fibonacci level of $61.16.

Dusit, a Thai hotels and hospitality group, is entering Myanmar as others write off projects and close hotels, but on terms that limit its exposure: management contracts on existing hotels, a focus on business travel, and outside checks on the owner. If leisure demand stays weak, the owner bears most of the cost. Dusit’s main risk is reputational.

Dusit Hotels and Resorts is taking over three of Yangon's best-known hotels, even though the Thai group says it does not expect Myanmar's international leisure market to recover any time soon.

The company opened The Strand Hotel Yangon – Dusit Collection and ASAI Yangon Sule under its management last week. Dusit Hotel Inya Lake Yangon is due to follow, the company said.

"We recognise that Myanmar presents a complex and challenging operating environment, and we do not anticipate a significant recovery in international leisure tourism in the near term," Chanin Donavanik, group CEO of Dusit International, told Skift.

The Inya Lake project also marks a return. Dusit first entered Myanmar in 2002, when it took over management of the Inya Lake Hotel.

Dusit International has taken over management of three established Yangon hotels — The Strand Hotel Yangon – Dusit Collection and ASAI Yangon Sule opened last week, with Dusit Hotel Inya Lake Yangon to follow — while saying it does not expect Myanmar's international leisure market to recover soon. Group CEO Chanin Donavanik said Dusit is targeting established corporate travel demand and described the move as asset-light expansion into a neighboring country, with management contracts that leave the owner carrying the buildings and any weak trading. All three hotels share one owning company that Dusit has not named; Chanin said an independent third-party law firm ran due diligence on the owner and its business partners, a step that matters in a market where foreign hotel groups have faced scrutiny over owners' links to the military. The backdrop is a market far off its peak: Myanmar recorded 973,263 international visitors in 2025 against an official 1.8 million target for this year, versus roughly 4.7 million before the pandemic and the 2021 coup. Hongkong and Shanghai Hotels wrote off its stalled Yangon project with a $20.5 million impairment in February 2025, and half of Yangon's 12 five-star hotels had closed by mid-2023. New Discord Setting Will Let You Take Over Streamed Games

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Michael Manning (He/Him) has been enamored with video games since he first played the Sonic Classic Collection as a child. With decades of experience in gaming and a full tour as a Gaming Journalist, Michael is ready to step up to the plate. From AAA to the smallest passion projects, his motto is that every piece of ar

The battle of online chat platforms used to be very close, with a wide range of selections that friends all had to choose between before getting started. Nowadays, the vast majority of gamers are centralized on one platform, allowing that platform to keep them engaged with constant small iterations and improvements.

Deadlock is starting to suffer from the same fate as other free-to-play games, with bot farmers crawling from the woodwork.

Of course, that platform is Discord, an online group chat and voice call app that seems like it's always adding new bells and whistles. Recently leaked, Discord is apparently working on a new function that will allow viewers of a friend’s stream to ask them to “pass the controller”, handing over direct control of the game being played to whoever has requested it.

As magical as this system may sound, it actually isn’t all that far-fetched, serving as a major selling point for the app Parsec, which specializes in allowing players to connect to each other's PCs, allowing online play of typically local-only titles. Steam’s Steamplay system functions similarly, though it requires players to provide their own feed of the game.

Discord’s offering of the function could be a major game changer, consolidating multiple functions into an already centralized app. The issue of latency and ping will always exist, but the company is doing a lot to earn back the users who were shaken by their recent collaboration with Google to open up channel messages directly to searches.

Aimed at opening up servers that exist to share info and house massive communities, no real hiccups have occurred yet, even as users stay vigilant. Discord has utilized all kinds of different minigames and in-app connections to keep users centralized on the app itself, to a fairly mixed effect.

The inclusion of the token shop and various quests to allow users to obtain cosmetics and even Nitro time for free was a massive success, allowing Discord to basically incentivize users to sit through various ads and sponsored quests. Discord has made it clear they aim to turn the app into more than just a chat venue.

Nowadays, the vast majority of gamers are centralized on one platform, allowing that platform to keep them engaged with constant small iterations and improvements.

This function has yet to be formally announced, and hasn’t had any kind of estimated release date presented, but could seriously enable a lot of older co-op games that don’t have modern releases or online multiplayer. Alongside the huge amount of game preservation work that takes place inside of Discord servers, this is potentially one of Discord’s most impactful updates, for better or for worse.

The only issue that may creep up on Discord is the eternal hex of feature creep, the mixtape where developers focus on implementing more things instead of fixing and finalizing the things they have now. Discord has really focused on adding more, with some real dust appearing on basic functions like voice calls. Latency has been increasing, and it takes longer to join on average.

These issues are annoying, but nothing overly serious, at least for now. The worry is always that functions like this, that allow players to control input on other users' machines, could be maliciously abused one way or another, either through modifications or exploitable bugs.

This is the same type of worry that users had about the Google initiative, and while nothing has come of it yet, users find it safer to err on the side of caution. Discord’s history of data breaches is far from spotless, and this kind of direct access could lead to real abuse if not properly put together. The function is likely to roll out slowly through some form of beta, but until official word is out, all we can do is speculate.

United Airlines Bans “AI Agents,” Claims It’s To “Protect Customers”

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

United Airlines has just banned travelers from using “AI Agents” to book or service tickets (thanks to TravelinWilly for flagging this). That’s an interesting stance for United, easily the country’s most tech savvy airline, to take. So what’s actually going on here?

United Airlines has today updated its contract of carriage, which are the terms that passengers have to agree to when they book a ticket. In the section about prohibited practices at the airline, the carrier added the following:

The use of AI Agents to make, modify or service a Ticket, and/or to purchase any service or product.

The “AI Agents” term is defined by United as follows:

AI Agent means any software or service, including but not limited to an AI assistant, AI agent or AI-enabled browser that takes autonomous or semi-autonomous action, whether or not Passenger or another individual supervises or approves its actions, to discover, search for, book, purchase, ticket, service or manage any product or service offered by or through United, including flights, seats, upgrades, ancillary products and partner products, on behalf of or at the instruction of Passenger or other individual. AI Agents include but are not limited to: (i) AI assistants built into social media, messaging, search or other consumer platforms; (ii) AI-enabled browsers and standalone AI agents; and (iii) any third-party AI assistant that accesses United through a tool, application or connection provided by a travel agency or other intermediary.

United even put out a press release about this change, claiming that it’s making the change to “protect” customers. Here’s part of the explanation:

Sharing account credentials with personal, third-party, AI assistants can expose sensitive information, and actions taken by these tools may result in unintended purchases, account changes, or use of MileagePlus miles. Plus, these automated AI actions have the potential to violate FAA and DOT regulations – as an example, flight check-in requires human acknowledgment that bags do not contain banned substances like explosives.

United claims it’s taking steps to restrict this kind of access, both directly within its digital platforms, and indirectly via travel agencies and aggregators, to prohibit use by unauthorized, third party personal AI agents or assistants.

It goes without saying that use of AI is becoming exponentially more popular in recent times, and is a force to be reckoned with. It’s anyone’s guess just how far it goes, in terms of how much the average person uses it in their everyday life.

There are certainly aspects of AI that are incredibly annoying, whether you look at it as an individual or a business. Admittedly I’m biased, but I’m so tired of feeling like half of what I’m reading online (whether on social media or websites) is written by a computer and not a human. It even extends to things like TripAdvisor reviews.

It’s also frustrating how many people write 30 paragraph emails nowadays, which are clearly 90% AI. I don’t really understand how that makes anyone’s life better. As far as I’m concerned, just give me the bullet points you fed your AI buddy, and we’ll all be better off for it. Anyway, rant over, back to United…

I suspect United’s real motive here is that it doesn’t want AI automatically being able to track and reprice flights (and then issue vouchers for passengers), or being able to too closely compare overall value on different airlines. For that matter, it probably just doesn’t want a third party getting that much data on the airline, not to mention that all of this can take a considerable toll on servers, if this scales.

But here’s what I find most interesting — the way this rule is written strikes me as being incredibly broad, no? Not to repeat the quote again, but this is the part I’m referencing, in particular:

AI Agent means any software or service, including but not limited to an AI assistant, AI agent or AI-enabled browser that takes autonomous or semi-autonomous action, whether or not Passenger or another individual supervises or approves its actions, to discover, search for, book, purchase, ticket, service or manage any product or service offered by or through United, including flights, seats, upgrades, ancillary products and partner products, on behalf of or at the instruction of Passenger or other individual.

Any software that automatically “discovers” or searches for any product or service offered by United would include all award search tools (along with all kinds of other businesses), no? Is that actually the intent here, or does United just plan to selectively enforce rules? We did see Air Canada engage in a battle over this a few years back, so is United planning something similar?

United Airlines has today updated its rules to ban “AI Agents,” and the airline even put out a press release about it. The company claims it’s making this change to protect customers and their accounts, as AI bots could have unintended consequences.

Many people are understandably skeptical of that explanation, as I imagine the airline also wants to ban these kinds of things to help people reprice tickets, get better fares, etc. For that matter, based on how broadly the definition is written, it seems that even things like award search engines would be banned.

AI agents would have a terrible look to book ratio, which will drive up costs for the airline. This has been around for travel agents for years. If we shop too much but book to little, we'll get reprimanded. Since that can't be done with bots, they have to be stopped somehow from shopping around too much. A human can perform only a limited number of searches at a time, whereas bots theoretically could search indefinitely. That would bring server and CRS costs go through the roof.

This seems pretty clear to stop an AI agent that would auto rebook the flight as it gets cheaper.

Horse has bolted. UA might as well get used to the flood and find a way to integrate such searches into its business. we are quickly heading to a future where your personal bot will be shopping from the merchants' bots.

There seems to be little discussion of when an airline's website makes the mistake. For a long period, BA Avios award searches for AA flights always reverted to the prior day rather than the date selected. I assumed this had to do with the time difference but whatever. I can clearly state that BA took no responsibility for any bookings based on their website error. The actual penalty was diminimus so i and I assumed others let it pass.

People seem to think there is this gray area if a bot or AI makes a mistake on your behalf. If a bot books the wrong date, destination, class or number of checked bags, that is all on you. You will be the one stuck with the consequences.

The Witcher 3 Is Using AI for Geralt’s Voice, But There’s a Very Good Reason

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Covering the video games industry since 2017, with experience in news, articles, and reviews. Currently a writer at DualShockers and gaming magazines.

Artificial intelligence has become one of the most controversial topics in the gaming industry, and it's not hard to understand why. From studios experimenting with AI-generated assets to developers finding new ways to automate creative work, players have plenty of reasons to be skeptical.

However, CD Projekt Red has found a rather unusual way to use the technology in The Witcher 3, and this time, there's a surprisingly touching story behind it.

The studio has recently confirmed that it is working with an AI company to adjust Geralt's Polish voice in the upcoming Songs of the Past expansion, which raised some players’ eyebrows. But don't worry, Jacek Rozenek, the actor who has portrayed the White Wolf in Polish since the original The Witcher, isn't being replaced.

In fact, he's still performing all of Geralt's new lines himself. This time, AI is actually being used as a supportive tool and is simply helping his voice sound closer to the one players remember from the 2015 RPG.

Amanita Design, the Czech studio behind Machinarium, recently called out an AI-generated project made using assets from the game.

As reported by Game File, Rozenek suffered a stroke in 2019 that left him unable to speak for months. His recovery was an incredibly difficult process, requiring him to train his voice for six or seven hours every day before he could return to acting.

He eventually returned to the role of Geralt, recording additional lines for The Witcher 3's next-gen update in 2022. However, the process took a significant toll on him. Rozenek told Game File that he was exhausted after those recording sessions, despite having worked hard to regain his ability to perform.

Jacek Rozenek, the actor who has portrayed the White Wolf in Polish since the original The Witcher, isn't being replaced.

When CD Projekt invited him back to record dialogue for Songs of the Past, the studio noticed that his voice had changed considerably. It was now lower and rougher than it had been when he recorded the thousands of lines featured in the original game.

Since the new expansion takes place around the same period as the base game, hearing Geralt suddenly sound different between old and new dialogue could not make sense to the player’s experience. So, rather than replacing Rozenek, however, CD Projekt decided to find another solution. A quite smart one, actually.

The studio partnered with Respeecher, an AI company specializing in voice technology that it had previously worked with on Phantom Liberty, the DLC of Cyberpunk 2077. With their help, Rozenek recorded all of Geralt's new dialogue as usual, and the recordings were then processed using an algorithm trained on his previous performances.

CD Projekt Red has just released Patch 5.01 for The Witcher 3: Wild Hunt Remastered.

In other words, the technology isn't generating Geralt's lines from scratch or replacing the actor's performance. Rozenek still delivers the dialogue, including its emotions and acting, while AI helps bring his voice closer to the version players heard over a decade ago.

Most importantly, the actor gave his full consent to the process and expressed his trust in the developers. "The point is that this voice should be as close as possible to my voice from 12 years ago," Rozenek explained.

CD Projekt's localization producer, Mikołaj Szwed, also acknowledged the concerns surrounding AI in the industry, but believes this is an example of how the technology can be used ethically. He described the situation as a special case, emphasizing that the decision was made because of Rozenek's health circumstances.

And honestly, it's difficult to argue with the sentiment behind it. Rather than using AI to cut costs or avoid hiring a performer, the Polish studio is using it to help an actor continue playing a character he's portrayed for decades. Songs of the Past is currently scheduled to launch in 2027, and Geralt's Polish voice will once again be in the hands of the person who helped make him so iconic!

The Witcher 3 has made its way into Cyberpunk 2077 with new cosmetic items to equip and two melee weapons to wield, inspired by the legendary RPG.

Stanley Tucci’s Airplane Snack Is the Messiest Meal Ever

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Stanley Tucci isn't settling for pretzels and peanuts on his next flight!

Between busy airport security lines and delays, eating while traveling can be unpredictable. And sure, maybe you ate breakfast before leaving the house. But then suddenly it’s six hours later and you’re struggling to navigate your way to baggage claim with a headache and a growling stomach. Trust me, I’ve been there too many times!

Which is why I have one simple rule when traveling: Eat when you can. Packing plenty of plane snacks is important, but getting a full meal is really ideal. So when I saw the kind of food that Stanley Tucci brings on flights, I felt incredibly validated. However, I don’t know if I could manage something quite this messy in the air.

In a very charming TikTok video, Stanley Tucci’s wife Felicity Blunt records him as he unwraps his plane snack. I watched in shock and awe as he unwrapped aluminum foil to reveal a sandwich. But not just any sandwich—I mean, this is Stanley Tucci we’re talking about. He’s known for whipping up Italian meals for pop stars and cooking cozy comfort meals on a whim. It’s no real surprise then that he brought eggplant Parmesan on a plane!

His wife joked that they should “film a before and after” of his shirt. I mean, this sandwich looks incredible. It’s got tons of creamy marinara sauce and is piled high with thinly sliced eggplant and cheese. The bread is toasted and really looks like it’s doing the best job it can at containing the feast within.

Would I have the confidence to eat an eggplant Parmesan sandwich on a plane? Probably not. I am a notoriously messy eater to begin with, and if bibs were more socially acceptable for adults, I would wear one with every meal. So I can’t imagine diving into this on a plane!

But Stanley loves it. In fact, he’s the one who made the sandwich, or “Stan-wich” as Felicity calls them, for the plane ride. So maybe I need to amend my earlier assertion: I would eat eggplant Parmesan on a plane if Stanley Tucci made it for me.

What’s your go-to airplane snack? Is it as messy as this one? Sound off in the comments below!

JetBlue’s New First Class Is Missing A Cabin Divider… But Why?!?

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

In the coming weeks, JetBlue will be introducing a domestic first class product for the first time, which will be known as BlueFirst. This is an exciting and long overdue development, and I trust it will help JetBlue’s revenue performance, especially given how efficiently planes are being reconfigured (by reducing legroom in economy).

The first aircraft has now been retrofitted with the new cabins, and it appears that JetBlue had a media day today for this showing off both the new hard and soft product. We already largely knew what to expect, but what stands out to me is the one thing that’s missing, which I really can’t make sense of.

I don’t want to bury the lede here, so let me get right to it — on planes with BlueFirst, JetBlue won’t have any sort of a cabin divider between first class and the rest of the cabin. It’s not just that there isn’t a curtain, but there’s not even a divider hanging from the overhead bins.

JetBlue unveiled its new domestic first-class seats Wednesday as more of the country’s carriers bet on the premium travel boom to make them profitable again.Read more: https://t.co/5o3k2Ru5R9 pic.twitter.com/1BTcLVOr7s

I guess this also explains the very creative angles of the renderings that have been released up until now, which clearly try to avoid highlighting this reality.

I really can’t make sense of this decision. I know we no longer live in an era where there are real, full bulkheads between cabins, since that’s not efficient. But just installing a small, flimsy divider, along with a curtain, costs very little, doesn’t weigh a lot, etc.

To be clear, this isn’t about “oh, I don’t want to be seen by anyone.” Instead, it’s about managing traffic to and from the forward lavatory. At US airlines, the most common policy is that on domestic flights, you can use any lavatory onboard the aircraft, even in a different cabin. There are some exceptions, including for JetBlue Mint, but that’s also easier to enforce thanks to how the A321 is configured, with a lavatory behind Mint, in front of economy.

I’m not sure if JetBlue intends to reserve the forward lavatory for BlueFirst passengers, but either way, you can expect there will be a steady stream of passengers lining up to use the forward lavatory. So it’s not even about potentially not having a dedicated lavatory, but instead it’s about just constantly having people standing in the aisle right by your seat. This will also make the life of the first class flight attendant more difficult.

Take Aer Lingus AerSpace, for example, which is Aer Lingus’ premium product on short haul flights in Europe. It’s simply the first row of seats with blocked middles, and no divider between that and the rest of the plane. The problem is that if you’re seated in the aisle in AerSpace, you basically just have someone’s butt in your face the entire flight. You’re almost better off not booking the product, just to avoid that.

Anyway, I can’t really make sense of this decision, so I’d be fascinated to know what the logic was. There’s not another US airline with first class that has no separation between first class and economy. Spirit’s “Big Front Seat” didn’t have any divider, but that also wasn’t first class — it was just an, ummm, big front seat.

Similarly, Breeze Airways’ “Nicest” product also doesn’t have a divider, but it’s also not a separate cabin.

CNBC’s Phil Le Beau was onboard JetBlue’s first BlueFirst A320 with CEO Joanna Geraghty. I like Le Beau, but this interview is also the perfect example of why I just don’t really value interviewing airline executives in a traditional sense.

Le Beau asked Geraghty a good question — “did you wait too long to add a first class?” If we are being honest, there’s only one correct answer, which is OF COURSE. Like, unarguably, JetBlue was at least years late, if not a decade late, to introducing first class. That’s not even Geraghty’s fault, it’s that previous CEO Robin Hayes was perhaps one of the least focused CEOs the US airline industry has ever seen.

Geraghty’s answer, though, was “I don’t think we’ve waited too long, we’ve had Mint for a very long time, it just takes time with some of these products to roll them out.” I get it — nobody wants to be, like “we screwed up and waited too long, but I mainly blame the guy before me.”

Seriously, though, if she really doesn’t think JetBlue waited too long to commit to introducing first class, then I’d question how observant the executive team has been of the industry. I mean, Frontier announced it would introduce first class before JetBlue, which should tell you all you need to know. Oh, and Frontier will even have divider, even if it doesn’t have recline, food, or drinks!

Also, the below screen grab from the interview gave me a chuckle…

JetBlue’s BlueFirst class is entering service soon, which is exciting. I’m a huge fan of JetBlue, and I think historically they’ve done a great job with both their hard and soft product, and have great attention to detail. So I’m just a bit puzzled as to how they came to the conclusion that there was no need to introduce a cabin divider between first class and economy.

It’s not about “privacy,” or anything like that, but instead it’s about trying to minimize foot traffic, and people congregating in the aisle waiting for the lavatory. You don’t want to pay extra to sit in first class, only to have people standing in the aisle the entire flight waiting to use the bathroom.

JetBlue will be the first airline in the US with first class to not have any sort of a cabin divider, and I can’t make sense of that.

What do you make of JetBlue’s odd cabin divider decision?

I just noticed the Frontier seat backs extend over the center armrest...miss that privacy feature from the loungers of yore!

APOD: 2026 November 12 – Moon, Venus and Spica Meet

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Discover the cosmos! Each day a different image or photograph of our fascinating universe is featured, along with a brief explanation written by a professional astronomer.

Explanation: Not all those who wander are lost. A night sky example is that planets wander, and in fact the very word "planet" comes from "wandering star" in Greek. As planets shift relative to fixed stars, they may appear from our perspective to get closer to other astronomical objects. Such meetings are called conjunctions. They don't happen by chance, but follow the precise laws of celestial mechanics. This peaceful image, captured in Poland, shows the crescent Moon above the Tatra Mountains, and bright Venus close by, a little lower in the horizon. The bright star to the left of Venus is Spica in the constellation of Virgo, the Maiden. Spica is one of the brightest stars in the sky, only about 250 light-years away. It was found in 1890 that Spica is a binary star: two massive blue stars orbiting each other every 4 days. But earlier this year it was reported that Spica, the current companion of wandering Venus, is actually a triple system.Tomorrow's picture: what's next?

NASA explores the unknown in air and space, innovates for the benefit of humanity, and inspires the world through discovery.

6 Reasons Why Citi Strata Elite Beats Amex Platinum And Sapphire Reserve

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

I receive compensation for content and many links on this blog. Be aware that websites may earn compensation when a customer clicks on a link, when an application is approved, or when an account is opened. Citibank is an advertising partner of this site, as is American Express, Chase, Capital One, and others. Any opinions expressed in this post are my own, and have not been reviewed, approved, or endorsed by my advertising partners. I do not write about all offers that are available -- instead focusing on miles, points, and cash back (and currencies that can be converted into the same). Terms apply to the offers and benefits listed on this page.

Citi Strata Elite earns 50% more points on unbonused spending than Amex Platinum or Chase Sapphire Reserve, and those points can become American Airlines miles. It also costs $200 – $300 less per year.

I’ve written a lot about the card’s enormous first-year value. But the more I’ve used the hotel benefit, the more I think there’s a case for the Citi Strata EliteSM Card (See rates and fees) beyond the initial bonus. Here are six things it does better than Amex Platinum and Sapphire Reserve – both excellent cards and I have them both.

Banks advertise how many transfer partners they have. A long list can include many I even actively avoid (Aeromexico, Thai Airways). Most banks are also limited in whom they can work with because major airline and hotel cobrand card agreements usually include exclusivity provisions that limit what competing banks can offer. American Express has Delta. Chase has United. Citi has American. Of those three, I’d take American AAdvantage. Strata Elite points transfer to American at 1:1. And American still offers some terrific partner redemptions. However, some of my best American redemptions are just regular domestic coach trips. I’ve written about getting 4 cents and even 8 cents per mile from economy awards. American will sometimes make a flight cheap in miles while keeping the cash fare high. You don’t need to want a first class suite halfway around the world to get value from this program. Citi still has international partners I want, including Air France KLM Flying Blue, Singapore Airlines and Qatar Airways. Chase’s Hyatt transfers are a real advantage on the hotel side. (I still wish Bank of America had its own transferable points program, with Alaska’s Atmos Rewards as a partner.) 2. The Hotel Credit Works Beyond A Short List Of Luxury Properties Strata Elite offers up to $300 off prepaid hotel stays of two nights or longer booked through Citi Travel each calendar year. The discount comes off when you book, and applies to the room cost before taxes and fees. You can use it at ordinary hotels and independent properties, without restricting yourself to a luxury collection. Amex Platinum’s $600 annual hotel credit is split into two $300 periods and requires Fine Hotels + Resorts or The Hotel Collection bookings. Sapphire Reserve’s $500 The Edit credit is limited to that collection, with a maximum of $250 per qualifying booking. Chase also has an additional 2026 hotel credit, but that one is restricted to selected brands. Those programs can provide real value through breakfast, property credits and hotel loyalty benefits. Citi’s advantage is the much broader choice of hotels where I can use the discount. And I haven’t found that using Citi’s credit requires giving back the savings through a higher room rate. In January, I compared the Marriott at LaGuardia Airport: Citi Travel was $156 before taxes, while Marriott’s own member rate was $176. That’s $20 less per night before applying the credit. I still compare the room and cancellation terms, but Citi Travel has been competitive or even cheaper. I’ve also had little difficulty getting Marriott hotels to add my Bonvoy number at check-in and honor on-property benefits. Earlier this year I used the Strata Elite credit at a Marriott and received club lounge access. I similarly had no problem getting status recognition on a bank portal booking at a Westin this month. These third-party bookings generally aren’t supposed to be eligible for hotel points, elite-night credit or elite benefits. 3. It’s A Better Card For Everyday Spending Strata Elite earns 1.5 points per dollar on spending outside its bonus categories. Sapphire Reserve and Amex Platinum earn just one. You can do better with any bank pairing its primary card with a second card that offers different earn. Generally you’re permitted to pool those points. 4. Six Points Per Dollar On Weekend Evening Dining Is Fantastic Citi Nights earns six points per dollar at restaurants from 6 p.m. Friday until 6 a.m. Saturday, and again from 6 p.m. Saturday until 6 a.m. Sunday, all times Eastern. This works at restaurants generally, including qualifying restaurant delivery services. You don’t have to pick from a bank’s restaurant reservation list. Citi uses the time the purchase is authorized, so a Friday dinner doesn’t lose the bonus just because the charge posts on Monday. Outside Citi Nights, restaurant spending earns 3x. And Citi’s other big accelerators are 12x on hotels, car rentals and attractions booked through Citi Travel, and 6x on airfare booked there. You don’t earn points on the hotel spending covered by the $300 benefit. For airfare booked directly with an airline, Amex Platinum’s 5x and Sapphire Reserve’s 4x beat Citi’s 1.5x. Big Useful Credits Instead Of Many Small Ones I’ve laid out how to use the Strata Elite credits, but the basic approach is pretty simple: book a hotel stay and buy an American Airlines ticket. In addition to the $300 hotel benefit, there’s a $200 annual Splurge credit. Select American Airlines as one of your eligible merchants before purchasing, and a ticket billed directly by American qualifies. You don’t have to find incidental fees to use it on, as with Amex Platinum’s airline fee credit. The other choices are Best Buy, 1stDibs, Live Nation and Future Personal Training. You can activate up to two merchants at a time and use the credit over multiple eligible purchases. For me, American makes this easy. If the hotel benefit and Splurge credit replace $500 you’d have spent anyway, they leave just $95 of Citi’s $595 annual fee to cover. And both benefits run by calendar year, without dividing the money into monthly allowances. There’s also up to $200 a year for Blacklane, divided into $100 for January through June and $100 for July through December. 6. The Annual Fee Is Lower The annual fees are $595 for Citi Strata Elite vs $795 for Sapphire Reserve and $895 for Amex Platinum. You still get a premium travel card: Priority Pass lounge access with up to two guests, four Admirals Club passes each calendar year for eligible American or oneworld travel, travel protections, and up to $120 for a Global Entry or TSA PreCheck application every four years. And the first year still offers a lot. The current offer is 75,000 bonus points after spending $6,000 in the first three months. At 1.5 cents apiece, that’s $1,125 in points before counting what you earn from the spending itself. Someone opening the card now can use the hotel and Splurge benefits during the rest of 2026 and again in 2027, before their first anniversary. That’s up to $1,000 from those two benefits alone. If those credits replace spending you’d make anyway, the bonus plus the credits less the $595 fee comes to $1,530 in first-year value, before Blacklane, lounge access or points earned from purchases. Scotland’s Flow Country

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Blanket bogs in the Scottish Highlands store vast amounts of carbon.

Compared to ecosystems in many other parts of the world, the blanket bogs of the Flow Country in northern Scotland are unassuming—soggy, treeless landscapes dominated by hummocky mosses, grasses, and heathers. Yet, to ecologists, the seemingly desolate areas are nothing short of extraordinary.

The bogs store tremendous quantities of carbon. That’s because their soils are so waterlogged that microbes cannot fully break down dead plant material, a process that would otherwise release carbon dioxide. Instead, partially decayed plant matter accumulates into thick layers of spongy, crumbly, carbon-rich material called peat.

The unusual ecosystems, also called blanket mires and featherbed bogs, only occur in places with abundant rainfall, poor drainage, and low levels of evapotranspiration. These types of bogs cover just 120,000 square kilometers (46,000 square miles) globally, less than one-tenth of one percent of Earth’s land surface. The image above, captured by the OLI (Operational Land Imager) on Landsat 8, is centered on blanket bogs in the Scottish Highlands, home to the largest such ecosystem in Europe. This peatland is also the first of its type to be inscribed as a UNESCO World Heritage Site.

The peat bogs of northern Scotland, home to more than 30 species of sphagnum moss, are spread across some 1,900 square kilometers (730 square miles) of the country’s Highlands region. In this image, boggy areas appear dark brown and are sprinkled with lakes. The light green area on the right side around Thurso is farmland. The darker green geometric patterns in the center are conifer plantations. The image was captured in the spring, the part of the year when the land surface is most waterlogged and peat bogs begin to accumulate large amounts of new biomass.

While forests store most of their carbon in living trees—which eventually release it back into the atmosphere when they burn or die—peat bogs often store carbon for many thousands of years. Some parts of the Flow Country have been accumulating peat for more than 9,000 years, building deposits exceeding eight meters thick, according to UNESCO. Overall, scientists estimate that all of the world’s peatlands cover just 3 percent of Earth’s land surface but store more than 600 gigatons of carbon—about twice as much as all the world’s forests combined.

NASA Earth Observatory images by Michala Garrison, using Landsat data from the U.S. Geological Survey. Story by Adam Voiland.

Europe’s electric car sales jump 49% as global EV sales grow

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Global EV sales climbed 8% in October, with Europe’s battery electric vehicle (BEV) sales jumping 49% and North America posting its first year-over-year increase in 2026. But that North American rebound comes with a big catch.

Drivers bought 2.09 million EVs worldwide last month, according to new data from Benchmark Mineral Intelligence. That’s down 3% from September, but it brings sales for the first 10 months of 2026 to 17.5 million – up 5% from the same period last year.

Benchmark’s figures include both BEVs and plug-in hybrids (PHEVs) in the passenger-car and light-duty vehicle markets.

Europe sold 510,000 EVs in October, up 36% year over year. Fully electric cars did most of the work: BEV sales rose 49%, while plug-in hybrids grew 7%.

That puts October’s growth ahead of Europe’s 31% increase for the year so far. The region has now sold 4.5 million EVs in 2026.

Sales fell 17% from September’s record, but September got a boost from the UK’s twice-yearly registration plate change. The year-over-year figures give a clearer view of how quickly the market is growing.

Germany’s EV share topped 45% of new car sales, its highest level since December 2022. That earlier peak came as buyers rushed to get cars before subsidy cuts took effect. Fully electric car sales in Germany rose almost 80% from a year earlier to more than 95,000.

UK EV sales increased 30%. That’s particularly notable because the Electric Car Grant was already available in October 2025, although fewer models qualified then. So this growth comes against a market that was already getting government support.

And in Portugal, EVs accounted for 55% of new car sales in October, following a sharp rise in their market share over the past few months.

North American EV sales rose 24% year over year to 130,000 in October, the region’s first annual increase of 2026. Sales were flat from September, while the year-to-date decline narrowed to 21%, from 24% a month earlier. The region has sold 1.3 million EVs so far this year.

US EV sales reached around 110,000 units in October.

But here’s the catch: October 2025 was the first month after the federal EV tax credit expired. The credit ended for vehicles acquired after September 30, 2025, and sales dropped sharply afterward.

October 2026 is therefore being compared with a month when sales plummeted. A positive annual number doesn’t mean US demand has returned to its pre-expiration level.

“October is the first month compared against a period without the US federal tax credit, and the US market has stabilized at a lower level rather than recovered,” said Charles Lester, Benchmark’s data manager.

The flat month-over-month figure helps tell that story: Sales have steadied, but the market is operating from a lower base.

China sold 1.14 million EVs in October, up 5% from September but down 12% year over year. That annual decline was smaller than September’s 18% drop.

Sales through October reached 9.1 million, down 11%, compared with a 13% year-to-date decline at the end of September.

Even with fewer EVs being sold, their share of China’s new car market reached a fresh high. That’s because the wider passenger car market is shrinking faster.

Chinese automakers are continuing to produce vehicles and look overseas for buyers. Passenger car exports of new energy vehicles – China’s category covering BEVs and PHEVs – reached nearly 600,000 in October. That’s another monthly record, up from 558,000 in September.

Those exports face more pressure ahead. The EU has agreed to cap imports of Chinese-built hybrids, and China will reduce its EV export VAT rebate from 13% to 9% starting April 1, 2027.

Outside China, Europe, and North America, EV sales rose 85% year over year to 300,000 in October. Those markets have bought 2.6 million EVs so far in 2026, up 93%.

Australia’s sales jumped more than 200% in October, and EVs have gained market share sharply this year. But that hasn’t translated into healthy dealer profits, according to Benchmark. More brands, many of them Chinese, are competing for buyers, spreading sales across more companies and squeezing dealer margins.

Brazil’s growth has also been huge, although Benchmark cautions that some of its drivers are temporary. Importers brought vehicles in ahead of phased tariff increases, a fuel crisis pushed more buyers toward EVs, and BYD increased production at its Brazilian factory.

Nothing like consumer economics to drive sales. Local story about consumer economics: Windturbines on land is not popular. However, the locals realised they could heat their home for $300/year (in Denmark(!)) with these wind turbines and then they were tolerated! Same with cars - people gravitate to cheaper alternatives despite the more frequent charging and initial higher prices.

As the tariff increases take full effect and fuel price pressure eases, Benchmark expects those boosts to fade. BYD’s local production, which will also supply Argentina and Mexico, is set to become the main remaining support for that growth.

Should You Actually Add AI To That? A Four-Question Audit For Product Teams

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Victor Yocco, PhD, is a UX Researcher with a focus on AI and the author of Designing Agentic AI Experiences (CRC Press, 2026), Design for the Mind (Manning, … More about Victor ↬

Weekly tips on front-end & UX.Trusted by 182,000+ folks.

A product team at a large health insurer wants to add AI that writes the full written determination for benefits appeals specialists. The technology works as specified. However, the specialists stop using it, and the ones who keep using it take longer on each case than the ones who ignore it.

A short evaluation with the specialists surfaces the problem fast: reading an AI-written determination carefully enough to sign it takes about as long as writing one, and the specialist also has to check for mistakes they would never have made themselves. The feature adds work to a task these people had already mastered.

Rescoping the release fixes it. A second version surfaces the policy provisions and prior decisions relevant to each appeal and leaves the writing to the specialist. The search step disappears, review time drops, and the part of the job the specialist is good at stays where it was.

The technology is identical in both versions, and the difference is which part of the task the AI handles.

Every interface change costs the user something before it pays anything back. They have to rebuild their picture of how the tool works, learn where things are now, and decide how far to trust what the AI hands them. Nelson Cowan’s review of working memory research (2001) put the limit at about four items held in mind at once. All three of those jobs draw on the same small supply.

An AI feature in an enterprise software setting requires more from the user than a redesigned menu, because it changes what they do all day. Hand the synthesis to an AI and the user becomes a checker. Give it the recommendation too, and the job shrinks to signing off. Either change costs user attention, and for people whose training is their standing at work, either one can feel like a demotion. For example, in one study, Sophia Ackerhans (2025) and colleagues found that physicians asked to sign AI-assisted decisions reported a more serious threat to their professional identity, and explanations of the AI’s reasoning raised both trust and that threat together.

We can call that the break-even line. Returns to users come in three forms:

For example, an AI-powered fraud screening tool shows the errors-caught form at work, since the analyst reviews flags they would never have raised themselves, which costs attention, and the catch rate pays for it. That puts the feature above the break-even line.

Four questions can help your team locate the break-even line before design work starts. Each one produces a design condition the brief can act on.

These questions do not replace the build decision. Funding, strategy, and existing commitments all weigh on that, and four questions cannot see any of it.

Run through all four questions together, since no single question settles the issue alone. Do it while the feature is still a proposal, before anyone sinks weeks into design. You will also need at least one person who does the task for a living, such as a loan officer, analyst, or benefits specialist. They do not have to sit in the roadmap meeting, and a short conversation tells you where the mental effort actually sits and whether the feature would help or add work.

Meet Smashing Workshops on front-end, design & UX, with practical takeaways, live sessions, video recordings and a friendly Q&A. With Brad Frost, Stéph Walter and so many others.

Count everything the task asks of the user: the thinking, the clicking, and the checking they will owe once AI is involved. Fewer clicks proves nothing on its own, because a feature can cut clicks and still leave the person working harder. Write down the cost even when it goes up, since Question 2 may find a return that pays for it.

A loan officer reading 40 financial disclosures to find the three that conflict with lending policy is thinking hard, and AI that surfaces those conflicts cuts that demand directly. Compare that with typing a loan number into a tracking system, which costs a few seconds and almost no thought. Pre-filling that field relocates the cost instead of removing it, because the officer now checks what the machine entered. Conventional wisdom says to automate the repetitive and monotonous tasks, and the pre-filled field is where that advice misfires. In this example:

You can find the tasks worth automating by comparing total effort before and after. Count the checking the user inherits on the after side.

Who the user is matters as much as what the task is. One feature can clear the line for a junior analyst and fall short for a senior one. Hand a junior analyst an AI-drafted risk summary, and they gain a starting point they lack the pattern library to produce on their own. A senior analyst, however, reads that same summary against the analysis already formed in their head, spends four minutes doing it, and learns nothing.

A finding like that tells you who to ship to. Scope the addition to the group it serves.

Compare the AI approach against the user’s current method, including the spreadsheets and workarounds they built for themselves. Genuine ambiguity is one reason AI comes out ahead when someone explores options, produces a starting point, or looks for patterns too numerous to spot by hand. Scale, speed, and consistency across a group of people doing the same job are three more. A fourth is catching what the current method reliably misses, which is how the fraud screening tool earns its place. The advantage disappears once the user has a practiced route to a known answer.

For example, an HR team building a succession planning tool finds the answer running both ways inside one product. Using AI to surface internal candidates whose skill combinations nobody would think to search for wins on ambiguity and scale together, since no single right answer exists and manual review of that search space would take weeks. Drafting the notification email after someone picks a candidate loses to the method already in place. The format is familiar, the writing takes two minutes, and the person sending it holds firm opinions about tone. The candidate search earns the addition. The email draft layers an interaction onto a task that runs fine without one.

The nature of the input and the shape of the output are separate decisions. A task can hold genuine ambiguity and still call for a structured visual result instead of a conversational one. Pair this audit with a modality evaluation for the output side.

Every AI system makes mistakes. The question is whether your user will notice. Start with how visible the error is. Then weigh what it costs when nobody catches it, and whether the user still has the skill to catch it. Question 1 asks what checking costs. This one asks whether checking is even possible.

Error visibility varies enormously, and product teams tend to estimate it badly. For example, an AI trip planner that suggests a museum on the day it closes produces an invisible error, because nothing in the output looks wrong. The traveler finds out while standing outside a locked door in Tokyo. Compare that with a suggested flight that departs before the connecting flight lands, where the contradiction sits right there on the screen.

Automation bias makes the invisible cases worse. Raja Parasuraman and Dietrich Manzey (2010) reviewed decades of human factors research showing that people defer to automated output even when that output is wrong, across aviation, medicine, and process control. Trust climbs fast, and rarely as a conscious decision. Once it does, users stop applying the scrutiny that would catch the mistake.

Map the error surface, meaning the ways this feature can be wrong, before design work starts. List the three likeliest error types. Rate each one on visibility to the user and consequence if it slips through. Any combination of low visibility and high consequence becomes a design requirement.

Show users where the output came from and how the system produced it. That is provenance, and it is what lets trust settle at the right level. People who can see the source, the data behind it, and when it was pulled catch errors more often. Tell the traveler that a restaurant’s hours came from a page cached in March and they know what to check. A confident-sounding paragraph with no source attached leaves them guessing.

Letting the routine work go is a real benefit of adding AI. Nobody needs to protect a specialist’s ability to format a citation or an analyst’s ability to total a column by hand, and arguing to preserve skills the technology has absorbed wastes everyone’s time.

Still, the judgment that catches the machine’s mistakes stays exempt from replacement. That judgment makes every other delegation safe, and disuse degrades it like any other skill.

Research in radiology shows how fast a suggestion can override a reader’s own judgment. In a 2023 experiment, Thomas Dratsch and colleagues had 27 radiologists read mammograms alongside what they were told was an AI system (a simulation created solely for the experiment). The radiologists worked through a 10-image training set where the suggested category was always correct, then 40 more mammograms with incorrect suggestions planted in 12 of them. The result showed how incorrect AI suggestions damaged diagnostic accuracy at every experience level. The most experienced readers, averaging 15 years, assigned the correct category 82.3% of the time when the suggestion was right and 45.5% of the time when it was wrong, while the least experienced group fell from 79.7% to 19.8%. Experience softened the effect without preventing it. A year of deferring to the same system, with the judgment it replaced sitting unused, leaves a reader in a weaker position than the one these radiologists started from.

Question 3 looks at your user today. Extend the same test 12 months forward and you arrive at Question 4. Teams that answer the first and skip the second read the resulting failures as a quality problem in the model, when the cause is the human judgment they took out of daily use.

Ask whether this user could judge the quality of the output 12 months from now if the feature vanished tomorrow. When the answer is no, the design needs a mechanism that keeps verification skill in use. Periodic blind review works well and costs little, with the user forming a judgment before seeing what the AI produced. Rotating a share of cases off AI assistance does a similar job, as does a weekly comparison between machine output and a human-produced version.

Table 1 below gathers the four questions, the signals pointing each way, and the condition each one produces.

Table 1: The AI Addition Decision Matrix. Clear answers across all four questions point toward building the feature as proposed. A poor answer on Question 1 calls for a design condition or a change in scope, unless Question 2 finds a return that covers the cost. Questions 2 and 4 work the same way without that exception. A poor answer on Question 3 can stop the build.

Put the conditions in the design brief, where they become project requirements. Teams reduce AI features to yes or no because roadmap reviews demand it, and that throws away everything the audit found. A feature that answers well on Questions 1 and 2 and poorly on Question 3 proceeds only with provenance built in. Write that in on day one. Requirements that arrive at the end read as polish, and teams cut polish when timelines slip.

Let’s return to the insurer scenario from our opening. The first version of the tool fails Question 1. Writing a determination takes sustained thought, and the AI that produces the draft moves that effort into verification without reducing the total. An hour with the four questions would surface that before the first version reaches design.

Question 2 points in the same direction. Appeals determinations follow a familiar structure, and specialists reach the answer through a practiced route. The ambiguity in the task sits earlier, in finding which provisions and prior decisions apply.

Question 3 raises the stakes. A wrong determination affects someone’s medical coverage, and an error buried in fluent prose stays invisible until the next appeal arrives. That answer produces the provenance requirement in the second version, which cites the source document and retrieval date for every provision it surfaces.

Question 4 explains why the second version holds up. Specialists still write every determination, so the judgment that evaluates the retrieved material stays in daily use. Keeping version one would train specialists for a year to approve text they no longer have the practice to assess.

AI-generated meeting summaries sit inside products that millions of people open every day, and the audit reaches a different answer for them.

On Question 1, a summary removes the work of writing notes and adds the work of checking whether it attributed a decision to the right person. Total effort drops for the participant who never took notes. For the person who always took the notes and circulated them, the trade is closer, and the verification lands on them. That split is exactly what Question 1 is built to surface.

Question 2 compares the summary against someone typing while half-listening, which produces patchy notes. A summary beats that on completeness and on consistency from meeting to meeting, so the feature clears this question for most teams.

A summary that assigns a commitment to the wrong person looks exactly like a correct one, and nobody rereads the transcript to check. On Question 3, low visibility and real consequence together turn into a requirement for a linked source timestamp behind every action item.

The feature clears Question 4, and the design needs no mechanism. Nobody loses a skill worth protecting by giving up meeting notes, and the participants keep making every decision the summary records.

Two clear answers, one segment finding, and one condition. The timestamps ship with the feature, and the habitual note-taker is the segment the team checks after launch.

You can run this before the next AI feature reaches your roadmap.

Everett Rogers (2003) called this pro-innovation bias, the assumption that new capability means progress before anyone verifies the benefit, and competitive pressure turns a rival’s announcement into an internal mandate. An hour of structured questions gives your team a way to answer that pressure.

Audit results land badly when a designer presents them as reasons the feature should not exist. The conditions are the useful output, so bring those into the product review.

Start by naming the segment the feature serves, which makes the answer a yes for someone. Hand over the error surface as a requirement with a cost attached. A product manager can act on a request for timestamps behind every attributed decision, and nobody can act on a worry that users might not trust the output. Then offer the change in scope without waiting to be asked. A version that surfaces the provisions instead of writing the determination is a smaller build with a better adoption story, and that framing moves a roadmap conversation forward.

A sentence that works out loud: We think this clears the bar for junior analysts and adds a verification step for senior analysts, so we would scope it to onboarding and revisit the rest next quarter.

The attention a person can spend in a working day stays fixed no matter how good your model gets. Before the next feature reaches design, write down what it will cost the person doing the work and which of the three returns pays for it. With both answers in hand, your team can walk into the roadmap review with a scope to propose.

Colom’s historic win in Mississippi expands Senate majority for Democrats

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Attorney Scott Colom will be the state's first Black senator since Reconstruction.

(CN) â A surprisingly close U.S. Senate race in Mississippi ended late Wednesday with the victory of Democrat Scott Colom.

The results, which took more than a week to finalize as officials counted absentee and provisional ballots, capped one of the tightest and most-watched contests of the midterms. The election was eventually decided by fewer than 5,000 votes.

âFor months and months, Iâve been telling everybody everywhere I go that weâre going to win this election,â Colom said during a victory speech in Jackson Wednesday night. âBut every time I said it, I pointed out that the people were going to give me too much credit for it. Because itâs not something that I did, itâs something that we did.â

Colom will be the first Black senator to represent Mississippi since 1881, and the first ever directly elected by voters, rather than the Legislature.

The race unfolded amid a national Democratic âblue waveâ that narrowed or flipped several Republican-held seats, even as President Donald Trump had carried Mississippi by about 20 points in 2024**.**

Democrats now hold majorities in both houses of Congress: 55 seats in the Senate to the GOP’s 44, and 255 in the House, compared with 168 for Republicans. The Senate race in Alaska remains uncalled, but Democrat Mary Peltola has a slim lead.

Republican incumbent Cindy Hyde-Smith conceded Wednesday night with a video message on social media.

âWhile this is not the outcome we worked for or hoped for, I respect the decision of Mississippi voters. I want to congratulate Scott Colom and wish him well as he takes on the tremendous responsibility of representing our great state,â Hyde-Smith said.

Colom, 43, is a Columbus native and has served as a district attorney in the Northeastern part of the state since 2016. He was the first Black person elected district attorney in a majority-white district in Mississippi history after defeating a longtime incumbent. A Millsaps College and University of Wisconsin law graduate, he has served as a justice court judge and city prosecutor.

He campaigned on lowering costs, expanding healthcare access and public safety, emphasizing his prosecutorial record.

Colom closed Wednesday night with a pledge to work for all Mississippians.

âMy commitment to you, each and every one of you, is that, first, Iâm going to treat your children like I want my children to be treated. Iâm going to treat your parents the way I want my parents to be treated. Iâm going to treat your future the way I want my future to be,â he said.

Marvin King, associate professor of political science and African-American Studies at Ole Miss, said in a phone interview that the unexpectedly competitive Senate race reflected voter dissatisfaction with the incumbent, but not necessarily a lasting shift toward the Democrats.

âIt seems as if everyone was caught by surprise, well, everyone except Scott Colom,â King said. âI felt that Mississippians aren’t too different from the rest of the country in the fact that stuff’s gotten really expensive here, and telling people to change their lifestyle is not what they want to hear. You know, they want to hear a solution.â

Colom’s focus on the cost of living included an ad about beef prices that went viral. It shows Hyde-Smith saying, “You know, you have so many proteins to choose from.” “Pick another protein? I’d rather pick another senator instead,” various voters respond.

Nevertheless, King said the result was historically significant. He pointed to ticket-splitting â voters supporting Republican congressional candidates while rejecting Hyde-Smith â as evidence that some people were specifically turned off by the incumbent.

âIt’s a one-off,â he said, adding the election fell short of providing broader evidence that deep-red Mississippi is becoming a swing state. âWhat I see is this was Republicans who voted for a Democrat, not Republicans becoming Democrats.â
